# claude-fleet — product spec

This document is the single source of truth for what claude-fleet is and how it's built. The bar is rebuild-from-spec: a competent engineer (or Claude) reading only this file should be able to rebuild a functionally equivalent application.

See [`.claude/rules/spec-maintenance.md`](../.claude/rules/spec-maintenance.md) for the rule that keeps this doc honest.

---

## 1. Overview

**claude-fleet** is a desktop application for driving a small fleet (3–6) of Claude Code **workspaces** from a single window. The user picks a model (Claude via OAuth or API key, or a custom model endpoint from the app registry) and the app spins up a workspace (today: a Docker container backed by `dockerode`) — each gets a private host folder plus a fleet-wide shared folder under an app-level "fleet root" — running `claude` in a PTY, renders the live terminal with xterm.js, and surfaces structured observability (cost, tokens, tool calls, transcript history) sourced from the workspace's bind-mounted Claude transcript JSONL.

It is an operator console that runs on the user's machine against the user's local Docker daemon. It is not a multi-user service or a cloud product. Several nodes (desktops, and headless nodes) can share files through git-synced folders (§8 *Sharing folders between nodes*); the app never drives a remote Docker daemon.

**Terminology.** "Workspace" is the user-level concept the UI talks about: a named place where a Claude session runs against a directory. It's persisted on disk as a manifest at `<userData>/state/<id>/workspace.json` independent of any backend's lifecycle, so workspaces survive container deletion and can be restarted. "Container" in this doc refers specifically to a Docker-container backend; a **local** (non-container) backend that runs `claude` directly on the host is also implemented (#16, Linux/macOS). A workspace's `kind` selects which.

## 2. Goals

- Run multiple Claude Code sessions in parallel, each fully isolated in its own workspace with its own host-directory bind-mount.
- One window, one keyboard, one set of credentials — no juggling terminals or shells.
- Workspaces persist across backend lifecycle. Each workspace has an immutable ULID `id` and a mutable display `name`; the host-side manifest at `<userData>/state/<id>/workspace.json` survives container deletion and rename. The workspace modal's Saved tab lists every stopped / deleted workspace (the "cold" fleet — running/paused live in the top strip); each row expands inline into an edit form so the user can adjust the spec before the Resume button starts the container (looked up by `com.claude-fleet.id` label) — or recreates from the saved spec, reusing the same ULID so state-dir + vault history stay attached.
- Persistent **expert workspaces**: pause an entire workspace plus its session set, then resume later and re-attach to every session right where it was — same conversation, same in-memory context. Lets the user build domain-specific agents that load their architecture / documentation / codebase knowledge once, sleep when idle, and wake up ready to act (analyze a PR, answer a question, run a check) without re-priming the context every time.
- Live terminal fidelity: cursor, colors, resize, paste, scrollback — all the things xterm.js gives you.
- Structured observability layered on top of the raw terminal: per-session cost, token counts, tool calls, transcript history — read from the Claude transcript JSONL that the CLI already writes, not by scraping the terminal stream.
- A global, workspace-filterable table of past Claude Code sessions with auto-generated short descriptions — selectable to resume any session in any workspace, regardless of which workspace originally ran it. Sessions persist across workspace deletion; the table is the durable record of past work.
- Drop OS files, pasted images, web content, or text fragments onto the window and have them saved into the selected workspace's directory where the agent can read them. The window is the inbox; the path lands on the clipboard for the user to reference in their next prompt.
- A durable, append-only mirror of every event Claude Code emits, written to `<workspace>/_history/<session-id>.jsonl` so the agent or user can refer back to pre-compaction turns. Whether the mirror is written, and whether it survives an explicit "Close terminal", are per-profile defaults (factory: write the mirror, delete on close). Both defaults can be overridden — the write decision at open time, the cleanup decision in the modal at close time. The mirror, when written, persists across pane switches, workspace restarts, and app exits; only the explicit Close action prompts the cleanup question.
- An always-on, structured log of every prompt Claude makes to the user — permission requests, `AskUserQuestion` calls, and plan-mode approvals — captured to a SQLite table the UI can review. The point is to give the user a substrate for tuning `.claude/settings.json` permissions and CLAUDE.md guidance over time: read what Claude is repeatedly asking about, then decide what to allow, deny, or document.
- Claude inside each workspace can query the application's state DB (sessions, cost, prompts, events) through a read-only MCP server exposed by claude-fleet. The agent gets typed tools for common queries — enough to consult its own past sessions, summarize cost patterns, or audit what it's been asking the user about — plus a scoped `query` tool for ad-hoc SQL. Reads are confined to the caller's own workspace (plus any it holds a `read` grant over); the `query` tool enforces this structurally via a per-call in-memory snapshot, not via SQL parsing.
- Credentials never touch the renderer process or the host filesystem in plaintext. They live in the OS keychain and are injected into workspaces as environment variables by the main process.

## 3. Non-goals

- **Not a remote orchestrator.** The app never drives remote Docker, SSH or Kubernetes. A *remote workspace's* container lifecycle belongs to its deployment; the desktop only talks to that host's `fleet-host` API (§7 *Remote host*). Cross-node file sharing is git-synced folders, run as an ordinary automation.
- **One user per desktop; shared remote hosts.** A desktop has one user, machine and keychain. A remote host is shared by everyone its deployment grants, verified per request by GitHub identity.
- **Not a generic terminal multiplexer.** Every session targets `claude` in a managed container. No arbitrary shells, no `docker exec` on unmanaged containers.
- **Not a session recorder.** Terminal output is rendered but not persisted; durable history comes from Claude's own transcript JSONL, not from the PTY stream.
- **Not a Claude Code replacement.** The CLI inside the container is the source of truth for what runs. This app is a viewport and lifecycle manager around it.
- **No auto-updater, no telemetry.** The runner image sets `DISABLE_AUTOUPDATER=1` and `DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1`.
- **Not a general port manager.** Preview opens the system browser for ports an agent declares (`expose_port`) or the broker scan finds — no arbitrary `host:port` dialing, no LAN exposure, no in-app preview tab.
- **Not an inference manager.** Fleet workspaces consume model endpoints (local Ollama, org LLMs, or cloud services) but do not manage them. `docker/inference/compose.yaml` is a dev/test fixture only — no model downloads, no GPU scheduling, no container lifecycle as a product feature.

## 4. Stack

| Layer | Choice | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Shell | Electron | Native window with full Node.js access in the main process — needed for Docker socket, OS keychain, and SQLite. |
| Bundler | `electron-vite` + Vite | One config covers main/preload/renderer with sensible defaults; HMR for the renderer; no hand-rolled Webpack. |
| UI | React 18 + TypeScript | Standard, well-understood. TypeScript is non-negotiable given the IPC surface area. |
| Terminal | `@xterm/xterm` + `@xterm/addon-fit` | The de facto web terminal. Handles ANSI, resize, scrollback, paste, copy-on-select. |
| Docker client | `dockerode` | Promise-based wrapper over the Docker Engine API with first-class streaming `exec` attach (needed for live PTY). Avoids shelling out to the `docker` CLI. |
| Credentials | Electron `safeStorage` | Backs the per-workspace secret vault (`<userData>/secrets.enc`). Real OS encryption on macOS/Windows (and Linux with a desktop keyring). On Linux with no keyring (e.g. WSL) it opts into safeStorage's **plaintext backend** (`setUsePlainTextEncryption(true)` → base64, *not* OS-encrypted) so the vault is at least functional — which `keytar` was not (it failed outright). (`keytar` is retained only for the one-time purge of legacy entries in `migration.ts`.) |
| Local DB | `better-sqlite3` | Synchronous SQLite for the history/cost layer. Single-file, embedded, no daemon. JSONL→SQLite cache + cost rollup ship under #2; surrounding observability work (live push, slot consumers) follows. |
| Store layer | `kysely` | Typed async query builder over the local DBs (remote-workspaces spec §7 *One store layer*). The `inbox.db` stores (`InboxStore`, `NotifyStore`, `AutomationStore`, `ProposalStore`) are written once against Kysely and run on its `SqliteDialect` over the same better-sqlite3 connection; every store method is async. `state.db` reads and writes run on the same layer (`stateKysely()`, `src/main/db.ts`), with a prepared-statement cache (`sqliteKysely(raw, { statementCache: 256 })`) because the SQLite driver prepares every query and better-sqlite3 does not cache. The raw better-sqlite3 handle serves only `openDb()`'s synchronous open steps (`migrate()`, including its v12 `repairSyntheticFirstUserMessages` backfill, and the `pendingClaudeIds` rehydrate), plus a guarded close in `closeDb()` (`if (raw.open) raw.close()` — usually redundant, since `kdb.destroy()` already closes the connection through the statement-cache wrapper's own `close()`, but a safety net if that path ever changes); the MCP server's read-only connection and `searchTranscripts` stay raw (§11). Postgres is a second dialect of the same queries (`src/main/store/postgres.ts`): one `pg` pool shared by both stores, `int8`/`numeric` parsed as numbers, `search_path` set per connection, in-process writes serialized per store (read-write transactions and bare writes) while reads and read-only transactions run concurrently, and multi-query reads in `readTransaction` (REPEATABLE READ, READ ONLY on Postgres; a plain transaction on SQLite). |
| Managed Postgres | `pgvector/pgvector:0.8.7-pg16`, pinned by digest | The app-managed local store (§7 *Managed Postgres*): the same PG 16 + pgvector 0.8.7 the store suite and CI service run, so one baseline serves all of them (a unit test pins `build-app.yml`'s service image to the constant). The digest (`store/managedPostgres.ts:MANAGED_PG_IMAGE`) changes only with an app release; a new digest recreates the container on the same volume. |
| Postgres driver | `pg` + `pg-connection-string` | The `pg` pool behind the Postgres store (§7 *JSONL→SQLite cache*, *Postgres store*), 10 s connect timeout. `pg-connection-string` (pg's own parser, a direct dependency) finds a connection string's effective host for the TLS default (§9). Pure JS, externalized like every main dependency. Tests run on PG 16 with pgvector 0.8.7 (CI service `pgvector/pgvector:0.8.7-pg16`; `scripts/pg-test-server.sh` where Docker is unavailable). |
| RDS IAM auth | `@aws-sdk/rds-signer` + `@aws-sdk/credential-providers` | The `aws-rds-iam` password provider: a 15-minute SigV4 token minted per new connection, signed for the configured host and port as given (no alias resolution), credentials from the default Node chain (including EKS Pod Identity container credentials). Pinned at exactly `3.1162.0`, the release verified against a fake container-credentials endpoint (`passwordProvider.test.ts`). The token is time-bound, so the host clock must be synced (NTP). RDS checks it only at authentication, so open connections outlive it. `docker/scripts/rds-ca-bundle.sh <dest>` installs the AWS RDS global CA bundle for `verify-full`, checked against the sha256 pinned in `docker/versions.yaml` (the fleet-host image puts it at `/etc/ssl/certs/rds-global-bundle.pem`). |
| Embedder | `fleet-embedder` (Rust: `ort` 2.0.0-rc.10 = onnxruntime 1.22 statically linked, HF `tokenizers`) | Text embedding in a child process (`embedder/`, §7 *Local embeddings*) so the model's load and inference never run on the Electron main thread (an 18.7 s startup stall and ~250 MB of main RSS in process, #464). onnxruntime is linked in; the binary still depends on the platform C runtime (glibc ≥ 2.28 on Linux; the VC++ runtime on Windows, shipped beside it, plus the system DirectML/D3D12/DXGI/DXCore DLLs that the prebuilt onnxruntime imports, which sets a Windows 10 2004 floor). onnxruntime is pinned to the version that reproduces the in-process embedder's vectors (parity test); transformers.js + `onnxruntime-node` stay one release as its fallback. |
| File watcher | `chokidar` (Linux, and polling for WSL shares) / recursive `fs.watch` (Windows, macOS) | Tails JSONL transcripts as Claude Code appends to them, and watches claude's `sessions/<pid>.json` status files (peer-status reconciler, §5). These dirs keep every stale pid file, hundreds per workspace. Both watchers pick the backend through `NATIVE_RECURSIVE_DEFAULT`. On Windows and macOS, `RecursiveDirWatcher` (`recursiveDirWatcher.ts`) opens **one** recursive `fs.watch` per watched dir and does an async, depth-limited initial scan, emitting chokidar's `add`/`change`/`unlink`/`error` events. It watches `fs.realpathSync.native(root)`, because Electron on Windows derives event filenames by stripping the watched path's length from the long-name event path: an 8.3 short name in the root (`C:\Users\RUNNER~1\…`) otherwise yields filenames like `ce\a.jsonl`. Emitted paths stay under the root as registered. An event with no filename (Windows change-buffer overflow), or one whose path and parent directory both fail to stat, rescans the root as `change`. A root that fails to watch, either missing at `add()` or erroring later, is retried every 5 s until it watches or is unwatched. chokidar's non-polling mode opens a native watch handle per file and directory, each one a synchronous syscall on Windows, and that froze main for ~30 s at launch with thousands of transcripts and pid files (#464). Linux keeps chokidar (cheap inotify watches). The `\\wsl.localhost` poll watcher is always chokidar with `usePolling` (9P delivers no change events). chokidar is loaded via dynamic `await import('chokidar')` because v5 is ESM-only and the main bundle is CommonJS. |
| Unit tests | `vitest` | Fast, Vite-native runner for pure-TS modules (e.g., `pricing.ts`). Picks up `*.test.ts` next to source. E2E lives in `tests/` under Playwright. |
| Perf telemetry | `@opentelemetry/api` + `sdk-trace-base` + `sdk-metrics` + `context-async-hooks` + `@opentelemetry/core` + OTLP/HTTP trace + metric exporters | OTel-native instrumentation in the main process: the same spans and metrics feed the local `perf_events` SQLite store and any standard OTLP backend. The API no-ops when no provider is registered (recording off), so instrumentation sites are effectively free while disabled. context-async-hooks registers the AsyncLocalStorage context manager that parents nested spans. |

**Native modules.** `better-sqlite3`, `keytar` (retained for the legacy-purge migration), `node-pty`, and `onnxruntime-node` (the in-process fallback embedder — see §7 *Local embeddings*) ship as native bindings — they must match Electron's bundled Node ABI, not the system Node. The repo's `postinstall` script runs `electron-builder install-app-deps` to pull prebuilt binaries (or rebuild) for the current Electron version. Without this hook, `npm install` builds the bindings against the system Node and Electron fails to load them at runtime with a `NODE_MODULE_VERSION` mismatch. (The vault itself no longer needs a native module — `safeStorage` is built into Electron.) **`electron-builder.yml` must `asarUnpack` these native modules** (`**/*.node` plus the `better-sqlite3`/`keytar`/`node-pty`/`onnxruntime-node` trees): a `.node` binary cannot be `dlopen`'d from inside `app.asar`, so without this the packaged app crashes the moment it touches SQLite, the keychain, a PTY, or the embedding pipeline. electron-builder's automatic smart-unpack does **not** reliably catch all four, so the unpack list is declared explicitly. Runtime deps in the packaged main process must also be loaded through the **CJS loader**: `@huggingface/transformers` is required lazily via `createRequire` (its `dist/transformers.node.cjs` entry), never dynamic `import()` — Electron's ESM loader cannot resolve packages from inside `app.asar`, and a dynamic import that works in dev fails in every packaged build with a mangled `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND` (#194); a regression test in `embeddings.test.ts` pins this. The transitive `sharp` dep **must ship and be asarUnpacked** (full `sharp` + `@img/*` trees — sharp's `.node` binding dlopens the libvips shared libraries relative to its own real path): transformers' CJS node build imports sharp at module load (`pipelines.js` → `utils/image.js`), so excluding it breaks every embedder load in the packaged app with `Cannot find module 'sharp'` even though text feature-extraction never uses it. An earlier revision excluded sharp to save size; that was the root failure behind the packaged `search_transcripts` breakage (#194 fallout). Size stays bounded because npm installs only the build platform's `@img/*` optional packages.

**Embedder sidecar binary.** `fleet-embedder[.exe]` is not a Node module. `embedder/scripts/ci-build.sh` builds it natively in the `build-app` and `release` build jobs (toolchain pinned by `embedder/rust-toolchain.toml`) into `embedder/bin/`, which `electron-builder.yml` ships via `extraResources` as `resources/embedder/`, and checks it. **Linux:** `embedder/scripts/ci-build-linux.sh` runs the script in a `rust:1.91-bookworm` container (GCC 12, glibc 2.36), because a newer distro's `libstdc++.a` references glibc symbols 2.28 lacks; the image is pinned by digest, and `embedder/scripts/install-zigbuild.sh` fetches zig 0.14.1 and cargo-zigbuild 0.24.1 there with pinned sha256s (x86_64 only: any other arch exits with `unsupported arch`; `install-zigbuild.test.sh` checks the selection). `FLEET_GLIBC_COMPAT=1` (set by the script) makes `build.rs` compile the shims and link libstdc++ statically; a plain dev `cargo build` does neither. The script downloads the prebuilt static onnxruntime itself (URL and sha256 from ort-sys's `dist.txt`; the crate's default `download-binaries` feature is off there, since zig cannot compile its OpenSSL dependency) and builds with `cargo zigbuild --no-default-features --target <triple>.2.28` (zig's versioned glibc stubs; the triple is `$(uname -m)-unknown-linux-gnu`, and one without a prebuilt onnxruntime in `dist.txt` fails the build) with libstdc++ linked statically (`build.rs`, with `ORT_CXX_STDLIB=` set by the script so ort-sys doesn't also link it dynamically), and `embedder/glibc_compat.c` defines the newer-glibc symbols the prebuilt onnxruntime and libstdc++ reference (the C23 `__isoc23_*` strto*/wcsto*/sscanf entry points, `__libc_single_threaded`, `gettid`); `embedder/scripts/check-glibc.sh` fails the job if any symbol needs a glibc above 2.28 or libstdc++/libgcc_s is linked dynamically, and the parity test (CI `unit-run`) runs against that same binary. **Windows:** the prebuilt onnxruntime is built `/MD`, so `msvcp140{,_1}.dll` and `vcruntime140{,_1}.dll` are copied app-local beside the exe (Windows searches the exe's directory first); it also imports `DirectML.dll`, `d3d12.dll`, `dxgi.dll` and `dxcore.dll`, which are Windows system DLLs; `dxcore.dll` first shipped in Windows 10 2004 (build 19041), so older Windows can't load the exe and the app embeds in process. The exe and DLLs are zipped as `fleet-embedder-windows-x64.zip`, uploaded as the `fleet-embedder-windows` artifact and attached to the draft release by the release job. `make embedder-win` (a dependency of `make windows`/`make portable`) removes any previous exe/DLLs, then downloads that zip from a GitHub release (`EMBEDDER_TAG`; by default `v<package.json version>`, else the newest release, drafts included; it prints the tag it used) or uses `EMBEDDER_WIN_ZIP=<path>`; it fails unless `ALLOW_NO_EMBEDDER=1`. Cross-compiling from WSL (cargo-xwin) would need the MSVC SDK. **macOS:** the linker's ad-hoc signature must survive `strip`, since arm64 macOS kills unsigned code; the build and the packaged `.app` are both checked with `codesign -v --strict`. Every build ends with a smoke run (`--model-dir does-not-exist` must exit 1 with a `fatal` frame), and the build jobs then run `embedder/scripts/smoke-embed.mjs`, a real embed of two texts with the cached model (unit norms, distinct vectors). A build without the binary still works: the app embeds in process. The host image's `embedder-build` stage runs the same two Linux scripts (§4 *Host images*).

**Cross-building the Windows installer from Linux/WSL.** A `Makefile` builds the unsigned Windows NSIS installer without a Windows machine (`make wine` once, then `make dist`). The packaging works because the three native modules all resolve to Windows binaries without a host compiler: `better-sqlite3` + `keytar` have Electron-ABI Windows prebuilds that `install-app-deps --platform win32` fetches, and `node-pty` already bundles its `win32-x64`/`win32-arm64` prebuilds in the npm package. Two Linux-specific snags are handled in the `natives-win` target: (1) `cpu-features` (an *optional* transitive dep of `ssh2` via `dockerode`) has no Windows prebuild and only compiles from source — it is removed (`ssh2` runs fine without it); (2) `node-pty`'s install script keys off the *host* platform and would try to compile on Linux, so a stub `prebuilds/linux-<arch>` dir makes `npm rebuild` a no-op and preserves its bundled win32 binaries. **Wine is required only for the NSIS target** — electron-builder generates the uninstaller by executing the freshly built installer (a Windows binary). The `make portable` target produces the unpacked app dir without NSIS and therefore needs no Wine. Code signing is out of scope for this path (the installer is unsigned; users see a SmartScreen warning).

**CI build matrix.** `.github/workflows/build-app.yml` builds the app on **native runners** (`macos-latest` → `.dmg`, `windows-latest` → `.exe`, `ubuntu-latest` → `.AppImage`). macOS/Linux use a plain `npm ci` — their toolchains compile/fetch native modules directly. **Windows must not compile from source**: `node-gyp` can't detect the runner's newest Visual Studio, so the Windows job uses `npm ci --ignore-scripts`, drops the optional `cpu-features` dep, and runs `install-app-deps --platform win32` to pull the Electron-ABI prebuilts (better-sqlite3/keytar) and use node-pty's bundled win prebuild — the same prebuilt-only strategy as the Makefile, minus Wine (NSIS runs natively on the Windows runner). `install-app-deps` still compiles node-pty there (~90 s), so the Windows build and e2e jobs share an `actions/cache` of the finished `node_modules` keyed on OS + `package-lock.json` hash. It runs on PRs (build + upload artifacts) and on `v*` tags (build, then publish a **draft** GitHub Release with the unsigned installers attached). A separate **`.github/workflows/release.yml`** offers the same draft-release output on **manual dispatch** (`workflow_dispatch` with a required `tag` input) so a maintainer can cut a draft on demand without pushing a tag — it mirrors the same build matrix, and the draft's tag becomes a real git ref only when the release is published (created at the built commit via `--target`). The Makefile remains the path for a local Windows build from a Linux/WSL dev box without CI. Note: `npm ci` is strict about lockfile/`package.json` consistency, so `package-lock.json` must stay in sync (regenerate with `npm install --package-lock-only` if `npm ci` reports missing entries).

The **base** runner image is `ghcr.io/<owner>/claude-fleet/runner:latest`, built from `docker/Dockerfile`. Base: `node:22-bookworm-slim`. Installs `git`, `ca-certificates`, `curl`, `ripgrep`, `jq`, `less`, `socat`, `tini`, globally installs `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` **pinned to a specific version**, and ships the Go **broker** (built in an earlier stage). Runs as non-root user `fleet` (UID/GID 1000 by default). Entrypoint is `tini`; `CMD` is the broker (the long-running PID 1). The base is deliberately **lean and standalone**.

The image bakes four hook artifacts into `/usr/local/lib/claude-fleet/`:
- **`input-wait-report.sh`** — calls `signal_input_wait` on `PreToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` (`waiting=true`), and on `PostToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` / `Stop` / `UserPromptSubmit` (`waiting=false`).
- **`session-report.sh`** — `SessionStart` hook; reads `session_id` from the hook payload and `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_SESSION_ID` from env, then calls `report_session_mapping` over the MCP socket. See §7 *Broker env-var contract* for the verified-mapping and drift-correction rationale.
- **`summarize.sh`** — `Stop` hook; debounced LLM chapter-summary generator. See §4 *Session-summary hook* for the full description.
- **`summary-core.sh`** — sourced library holding the shared chapter-generation core (`summarize_next_chunk`): chunk-bounded window slicing, watermark claim, model call, strict-JSON validation, sidecar append, breadcrumbs. Sourced by `summarize.sh` and `backfill-summaries.sh` so the live and backfill paths share one windowing implementation (#246).
- **`backfill-summaries.sh`** — `SessionStart` hook; sweeps the workspace's transcripts for sessions with ≥ `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_NEW_TURNS` unsummarized turns past their watermark (sessions that ended before the pipeline worked) and drains them in organic-cadence chunks under a per-sweep budget. See *Backfill sweep* below.
- **`hooks.settings.json`** — a claude settings file that registers all scripts for their respective hook events. The complete hook registrations: `PreToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` → `input-wait-report.sh`; `PostToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` → `input-wait-report.sh`; `Stop` → `input-wait-report.sh` + `summarize.sh`; `UserPromptSubmit` → `input-wait-report.sh`; `SessionStart` → `session-report.sh` + `backfill-summaries.sh`.

Every in-container `claude` launch passes `--settings /usr/local/lib/claude-fleet/hooks.settings.json` — a **trusted load** that bypasses the `/hooks` approval gate — so all hooks fire without user confirmation. They do not affect the user's own `.claude/settings.json`.

**Session-summary hook (`summarize.sh`).** A `Stop` hook runs `docker/runner/summarize.sh`, which appends chapter-summary events to a sidecar file `<uuid>.fleet.jsonl` (never the live transcript — appending to it corrupts `--resume`). The hook counts *completed turns* — typed human prompts with string content (tool-result arrays don't count) — against a persisted state file `<uuid>.fleet.state`. It re-summarizes only when **≥ `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_NEW_TURNS` new turns** have landed since the last chapter **and ≥ `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_INTERVAL_S` seconds** have elapsed; on both default values this means the haiku call fires at most once per ~20 prompts and once per ~2 minutes. When both conditions hold, the hook catches up in **chunks**: each iteration summarizes only the next `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_NEW_TURNS` turns past the watermark (window text capped to `CF_SUMMARY_WINDOW_CHARS` chars), claims the watermark at the chunk boundary *before* the model call, and repeats — up to `CF_SUMMARY_MAX_CHAPTERS_PER_RUN` chapters per run (default 5) — so a resumed session with a large backlog gets real chapters instead of one tail-truncated mega-window (#246). `fromEventTs`/`toEventTs` on each chapter are the **window's** first/last transcript timestamps (previously the whole transcript's — which gave every chapter identical stamps and would have poisoned Phase-3 decay scoring). A chunk that attempts but fails to generate (rejected output / empty window) ends the run; its turns are not retried (claim-first semantics, unchanged). The shared windowing logic lives in `summary-core.sh` (`summarize_next_chunk`), which builds the prompt in hijack-resistant order (#230): a framing line, the previous chapter's summary as a one-line continuity hint ("Previously: …"), then the window quoted as **delimited DATA** between `<<<TRANSCRIPT-WINDOW` / `TRANSCRIPT-WINDOW>>>` markers, and the operative instruction + strict-JSON output format **after** the window — with weak summarizer models whatever comes last wins, and a window ending in conversational text otherwise makes the model continue the transcript's conversation instead of summarizing. It calls `claude -p --model $CF_SUMMARY_MODEL` (run from `/tmp` so the throwaway transcript does not land in the watched directory), extracts the JSON object from the reply — the substring from the first `{` to the last `}`, which tolerates the markdown ```` ```json ```` code fence and any prose models (notably haiku) wrap around it — then demands strict JSON `{"summary":"…","tags":["…"],"title":"…"}`, validating `summary`+`tags` with jq. An invalid reply gets **exactly one retry** (hijacks and format slips are stochastic, and claim-first watermarking means a rejected chunk is skipped permanently — one retry is cheap insurance); only a second invalid reply reports `rejected` (`attempts:2`). On success it appends `{"type":"session-summary","summary","tags","sessionId","model","fromEventTs","toEventTs"}` to the sidecar. **Title refresh (#170):** the same reply also carries a short rolling `title`; when present (non-blank string) the hook appends a second `{"type":"ai-title","aiTitle","sessionId"}` line to the sidecar, which `ingestBatch` folds last-write-wins over `sessions.ai_title` — overwriting Claude Code's stale native **one-shot** title so the session tab (via `summaryForBrokerSession`) and the left-rail (same DB column) re-title as the session evolves. `title` is optional — an absent/blank field just skips the ai-title line (older prompts stay compatible) — and costs no extra LLM call since it piggybacks the existing chapter summarization. The background invocation (a detached `( … ) &` subshell) is fire-and-forget; the hook always exits 0 and the parent claude is never blocked.

**Diagnostics (#230).** Because the hook fails into `/dev/null` and is backgrounded, a broken pipeline is otherwise invisible (a whole workspace can accumulate zero chapters with no signal). The hook reports each decision point to the fleet-state MCP tool `report_summary_status` (same socket/token transport as `session-report.sh`), which the host lands in the `errors` table (reachable via `list_errors`). Always-on phases — rare, so no noise: `attempt` (gate passed, window non-empty, about to call the model), `generated` (chapter appended to the sidecar), `rejected` (model output failed strict-JSON validation, `warn` level), `empty-window` (gate passed but the sliced window was empty). One opt-in phase behind `CF_SUMMARY_DIAG=1` — `gate`, carrying `{turns,newTurns,minTurns}` on every below-threshold Stop, which reveals an in-container turn count that never climbs (e.g. jq under-counting the real transcript). Reading the phases disambiguates the failure layer: `attempt` with no following `generated`/`rejected` ⇒ the `claude -p` call hung/crashed; `generated` present but no chapter in the DB ⇒ sidecar-ingestion failure; no `attempt` on a many-turn session (with `gate` ticks showing a low count) ⇒ the turn count isn't computed in-container. A reporting failure never blocks or fails the hook. Test seam: `CF_SUMMARY_STATUS_SINK` captures the JSON-RPC requests instead of sending them.

**Backfill sweep (`backfill-summaries.sh`, #246).** Chapter generation historically required a live `Stop` hook, so sessions that ended while the pipeline was broken (or predate it) stayed unsummarized forever. On `SessionStart` the sweep backgrounds itself immediately (never delays the session), takes a `mkdir` lock (`/tmp/cf-backfill.lock`, stolen if older than an hour) so multi-tab wakes don't double-scan, and scans every `~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl` (skipping `*.fleet.jsonl` sidecars and the just-started session — the Stop hook owns that one), newest-mtime first. Transcripts with at least one full chunk of unsummarized turns are drained via the same `summarize_next_chunk` core, under a global per-sweep budget: `CF_BACKFILL_MAX_PER_SWEEP` chapters (default 10) with `CF_BACKFILL_DELAY_S` seconds between model calls (default 3); the backlog finishes draining across subsequent wakes. A chunk that attempts but fails to generate aborts the **entire sweep** (not just that transcript) — a broken model must not burn the remaining budget; the next wake retries from the advanced watermarks. `CF_BACKFILL=0` disables the sweep. Backfilled chapters are indistinguishable from organic ones (same cadence, tags, rolling `ai-title` — old sessions get retro-titled) and reach the DB through the normal sidecar-watcher path, which ingests idempotently. Sub-threshold tails stay unsummarized (organic parity) and covered turns are never re-summarized. Two always-on breadcrumbs frame each sweep: `backfill-start` `{candidates, backlogTurns}` and `backfill-done` `{generated, remaining, budget}`, attributed to the triggering session.

**Tunables** (workspace env, settable via the app's env-var editor — no new UI required): `CF_SUMMARY_MODEL` (default `haiku`), `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_NEW_TURNS` (default `20`), `CF_SUMMARY_MIN_INTERVAL_S` (default `120`), `CF_SUMMARY_WINDOW_CHARS` (default `8000`), `CF_SUMMARY_MAX_CHAPTERS_PER_RUN` (default `5`), `CF_BACKFILL` (default on; `0` disables), `CF_BACKFILL_MAX_PER_SWEEP` (default `10`), `CF_BACKFILL_DELAY_S` (default `3`). The `get_config` MCP tool returns the effective values (app defaults overridden by the workspace's env) so agents can inspect the knobs; it notes that manually changing env inside the container is not reflected until recreate.

**`summarize.sh` and `backfill-summaries.sh` are registered in `hooks.settings.json`** alongside `input-wait-report.sh` and baked into the runner image. The registered hooks in `hooks.settings.json` are now: `PreToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` → `input-wait-report.sh`; `PostToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` → `input-wait-report.sh`; `Stop` → `input-wait-report.sh` + `summarize.sh`; `UserPromptSubmit` → `input-wait-report.sh`; and `SessionStart` → `session-report.sh` + `backfill-summaries.sh` (see env-var contract below).

**DevOps image** (`…/claude-fleet/runner-devops`, `docker/devops/Dockerfile`) is built **`FROM` the base** and layers the SumerSports platform-engineering toolset: GitHub CLI, `yq`, tenv + OpenTofu, Terramate, OPA, tflint + trivy, kubectl + kustomize, helm, `dnsutils` (`dig`), and the AWS CLI; the **Azure CLI is opt-in** (`--build-arg INSTALL_AZURE_CLI=true` — its pip tree ~doubles image size, so it's off by default). The image carries **capability labels** (`com.claude-fleet.capabilities`, `com.claude-fleet.cloud=aws=…/azure=…`, `com.claude-fleet.variant=devops`) that the app surfaces as searchable chips in the workspace image picker, so you can find "the one with kubectl/aws/tofu". **Committee experts/managers run on this image** — their loadouts reach for `gh`/IaC tools. Install logic lives in **shared, standalone, arch-aware scripts under `docker/scripts/`** (one per toolset) that any image composes; pinned versions live in `docker/versions.yaml` (org-canon pins mirror `ci-images`). All images build with the **repo root as context** (so `docker/scripts/` + the broker module are reachable; `.dockerignore` opts those paths in). The publish workflow (`publish-runner.yml`) is **native multi-arch**: each architecture builds on its own **native runner** (`amd64` on `ubuntu-24.04`, `arm64` on `ubuntu-24.04-arm` — no QEMU emulation), pushing each arch **by digest** (no tag); a per-image **merge** job then stitches the arch digests into one tagged multi-arch manifest via `docker buildx imagetools create`. The stages are `base-build` (arch fan-out) → `base-merge` → `variant-build` (**variant × arch** fan-out, each `FROM` the base manifest digest via `--build-arg BASE_IMAGE=<base digest>` so they stay in lockstep) → `variant-merge`. Everything within a stage runs in parallel, and variants only wait on `base-merge`. Adding a variant is a new entry in the `variant-build`/`variant-merge` matrices (`variant`/`image`/`file`/optional `smoke`), not another sequential step. Future images stack the same way (e.g. a `platform` image `FROM` devops adding Java/.NET).

**Host images** (`…/claude-fleet/runner-host` FROM the base, `…/claude-fleet/runner-devops-host` FROM `runner-devops` of the same commit; `docker/host/Dockerfile`; `linux/amd64` only, since no node group runs arm64) add `fleet-host` (§7 *Remote host*):
- `/usr/local/lib/fleet-host/` holds the Vite bundle for plain Node 22 (`vite.host.config.ts`, `npm run build:host`): two entries, `fleet-host.mjs` and `fleet-host-cred.mjs`, plus shared and lazily loaded chunks under `chunks/` (the AWS SDK's credential providers), so the image has no `node_modules`. `better-sqlite3` is aliased to a stub that throws (`src/host/sqliteStub.ts`): the host store is Postgres only. A build stage runs `npm ci --ignore-scripts` and the bundle.
- `/usr/local/bin/fleet-host` wraps it (`fleet-host init`, `bootstrap`, `migrate`); `/usr/local/bin/fleet-host-entry` is the `CMD`, run by the base's tini. `/usr/local/bin/fleet-host-cred` wraps the credential client (§7 *Remote host*, *GitHub App credentials*).
- `/etc/gitconfig`, for `credential.https://github.com`: `helper =` (resets inherited helpers), then `helper = !fleet-host-cred git-credential`, `useHttpPath = true`. `/usr/local/lib/fleet-host/bin/gh`, first on `PATH`, is a `gh` shim that exports a repo-scoped `GH_TOKEN` and execs `/usr/local/bin/gh`. `/run/fleet-host` (0700, `fleet`) holds the credential socket. `/usr/local/lib/fleet-host/mcp-bridge.cjs` is the container MCP bridge, written at build by `fleet-host write-mcp-bridge <path>` from the desktop's `mcpContainerBridge.ts` (one source). `/run/fleet` (0700, `fleet`) holds the fleet-state MCP socket in the single-container layout; the container split mounts its own directory there (§7 *Remote host*, *Host MCP*).
- `/etc/ssl/certs/rds-global-bundle.pem` is the AWS RDS global CA bundle, fetched with `ADD --checksum=sha256:…`. AWS re-issues the file when it adds CAs; the pinned checksum is updated with it.
- **The embedder** (§7 *Remote host*, *Embedder*), in `/usr/local/lib/fleet-host/embed/` (`FLEET_HOST_EMBED_DIR`), about +61 MB per image:
  - Stage `embedder-build` is `FROM` the same digest-pinned `rust:1.91-bookworm` as `embedder/scripts/ci-build-linux.sh` (bump both together) and runs the desktop's Linux recipe, `install-zigbuild.sh` then `ci-build.sh` (§4 *Embedder sidecar binary*). The glibc 2.28 floor isn't needed in the image (Debian 12, glibc 2.36); it is kept so both binaries come from one recipe.
  - Stage `embed-weights` fetches only the two files the sidecar reads, `tokenizer.json` and `onnx/model_quantized.onnx`, from `huggingface.co/Xenova/bge-small-en-v1.5/resolve/<EMBED_HF_REVISION>/` (a Dockerfile `ARG`, an HF commit SHA, not a branch), and runs `sha256sum -c` against the committed `docker/host/embed-weights.sha256`; a mismatch fails the build. The host never fetches model files at run time.
  - Layout: `fleet-embedder` (0755, root); `model/tokenizer.json`, `model/onnx/model_quantized.onnx` and the manifest as `model/SHA256SUMS` (0644, root), so `sha256sum -c` can be re-run in a pod; `reference.json` (0644, root), a copy of `embedder/reference/bge-small-q8.json`: the 40 parity-corpus texts and their transformers.js vectors. The sidecar writes nothing, so any uid can run it under a read-only root filesystem.
  - The final stage runs `fleet-host embed-check` as `fleet`: it starts the sidecar exactly as the holder does, embeds the reference texts in batches of 8, and requires cosine ≥ 0.995 per text and a mean ≥ 0.9995 against the reference and every norm within 1e-4 of 1, printing `{ ok, n, minCosine, minIndex, meanCosine, revision, peakRssKiB }` (`minIndex` is the lowest-scoring text). Runner CPUs' int8 kernels differ by up to about 2e-3 per text (CI measured a minimum of 0.99932), while wrong weights or tokenization score at most 0.967. A broken binary, wrong weights, an unreadable file or CPU-kernel drift that `EMBED_MODEL_ID` can't show fails the build.
  - A model or revision bump changes `EMBED_HF_REVISION` (the Dockerfile `ARG` and `embedCheck.ts`), the manifest and the reference vectors together; `embedCheck.test.ts` fails when they diverge. The reference is written by the opt-in parity suite (`CF_EMBED_REFERENCE_WRITE=1`) on a machine whose model cache matches the manifest.
- Env defaults: `PATH=/usr/local/lib/fleet-host/bin:$PATH`, `FLEET_HOST_PORT=8080`, `FLEET_HOST_CONFIG=/etc/fleet-host/config.yaml`, `FLEET_HOST_STATE=/home/fleet/.claude/fleet-host`; uid/gid 1000 (`fleet`).
- **Supervision** (`fleet-host-entry`): runs the broker and fleet-host side by side. Broker exit ends the container (with the broker's status, after stopping fleet-host); fleet-host is restarted with backoff (1 s doubling to 10 s; PTYs live in the broker and survive), and the container exits only when fleet-host exits more than 5 times in 60 s. Exit 75 (`EXIT_DB_UNAVAILABLE`: the host DB is unreachable) is restarted with the same doubling backoff but never counts toward that limit, so a DB outage can't end the container and its PTYs. SIGTERM/SIGINT stops fleet-host first (so it releases the lease), then the broker, and never restarts. SIGHUP is forwarded to fleet-host. The script refuses to start when `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_TCP_PORT` is set, since TCP mode would serve the broker unauthenticated on the pod IP. Knobs: `FLEET_HOST_MAX_RESTARTS`, `FLEET_HOST_RESTART_WINDOW_S`, `FLEET_HOST_BACKOFF_START_MS`, `FLEET_HOST_BACKOFF_MAX_MS`.
- The bundle inputs are opted into `.dockerignore` (tests and `testFakes.ts` excluded), and so is `embedder/**` except `embedder/target/**` and `embedder/bin/**`; both image workflows' path filters list `embedder/**` and every `src/main` file in the host graph, and `src/host/importGraph.test.ts` fails when the allowlist, `.dockerignore` and the filters disagree. `runner-host` is excluded from `variant-build`'s arm64 leg, and `devops-host-build` builds amd64 only. `runner-host` is a `variant-build` entry; `devops-host-build`/`devops-host-merge` follow `variant-merge` and resolve `runner-devops:sha-<short>` to its digest. `docker/host/smoke.test.sh` (probes on 8080, `/whoami` 401, uid 1000, `fleet-host init`, `bootstrap` (usage, exit 2) and `migrate` (no `db:`, exit 1), the CA bundle, the MCP bridge file and `/run/fleet`, that the image's `claude` parses fleet-host's fresh-step argv (`--mcp-config=` takes one value, `--` ends options; checked against a missing config file, so no API call), the git credential helper and `gh` shim with the helper off, `fleet-embedder` is `755 root`, an in-pod `sha256sum -c` of `model/SHA256SUMS`, `fleet-host embed-check` prints `"ok":true`, no `fleet-embedder` process runs without `db:` (a `/proc/*/comm` scan; the slim base has no procps), a clean SIGTERM) gates both publishes on amd64, and `test-runner-images.yml` builds and smokes `runner-host` and runs `docker/host/fleet-host-entry.test.sh` pre-merge. Organisations extend a host image `FROM <pinned digest>`.

Because `publish-runner.yml` only runs **post-merge on `main`**, a separate **pre-merge gate** (`test-runner-images.yml`, on `pull_request` touching `docker/**`/`broker/**`) builds **every** image on a native amd64 runner and smoke-tests the ones that ship a test — **without pushing to GHCR**: the freshly-built base goes to an ephemeral **local registry** (a `registry:2` service container on `localhost:5000`) so the variants build `FROM` it. This catches build breaks (a bad install script) and runtime/PATH regressions before they can merge. It's amd64-only (native/fast; script and Dockerfile errors are arch-independent) — full multi-arch is still validated by the post-merge publish.

**Dev image** (`…/claude-fleet/runner-dev`, `docker/dev/Dockerfile`) is built **`FROM` the base** (not devops — it carries none of the cloud/k8s tooling) and layers a **polyglot dev toolchain**: the **GitHub CLI** (`gh`, shared `docker/scripts/gh.sh`), C/C++ `build-essential` (gcc/g++/make/pkg-config) + `libsecret-1-dev`, Python (`python3`/`pip`/`venv` + `uv`), Go (`/usr/local/go`), Rust (rustup into world-readable `/opt/cargo`+`/opt/rustup`), and the **Playwright browser system libraries** (browsers themselves install non-root at runtime via `npx playwright install`). This is the image for developing/building **claude-fleet itself** inside a workspace — it compiles the app's native modules (`better-sqlite3`, `node-pty`, `keytar`), runs `npx electron-rebuild`, and runs the e2e suite. Toolchains install as root at build time into **world-readable** locations (so any `docker run --user` UID reaches them on `PATH`); the container still runs non-root as `fleet`. Capability label `com.claude-fleet.variant=dev`. Version pins for gh/go/rust/uv live in `docker/versions.yaml`; the publish workflow builds it in the **variant fan-out** (pinned to the base manifest digest) and runs `docker/dev/smoke.test.sh` on its **native amd64** image (asserts every toolchain is reachable as `fleet`).

**Why claude is pinned, not `:latest`.** claude 2.1.150 added a "Managed settings require approval" startup gate that fires when an org pushes a privileged setting (e.g., `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_LOGS_ENDPOINT`) into the user's `remote-settings.json`. In 2.1.150 specifically, the prompt was unnavigable over our broker PTY — input bytes reached claude (verified via `/proc/<pid>/io` rchar growing and tty-echo coming back), but the prompt's parser didn't act on Enter, digits, or arrow-Enter combos, leaving every new OAuth workspace stuck before the main TUI. 2.1.169 fixed navigation and the pin currently sits at 2.1.281. Pinning the version protects against a silent re-regression in the same code path. Bumping the pin is deliberate — verify navigation works against a built image before raising the floor (see issue #65 for the test recipe and the broker probe script used to verify).

**Runner env contract — `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_MOUSE_CLICKS=1`.** The base image sets this env var (`docker/Dockerfile`). claude's fullscreen TUI (`/tui fullscreen`, or an org's managed `remote-settings.json`) switches to the alternate screen and enables SGR mouse tracking, which captures every click/drag/wheel — breaking native drag-select copy and right-click paste in the renderer's xterm.js terminal. claude's own clipboard fallback (`xclip`/`wl-copy`/`pbcopy`/PowerShell) can't run in the container (none installed, no display), so nothing rescues it. `DISABLE_MOUSE_CLICKS` (claude ≥ 2.1.195) stops the click/drag/hover capture while keeping wheel scroll, so xterm.js selection and the app's Ctrl+C→host-clipboard path (`TerminalSession.tsx doCopy`) keep working in fullscreen. Baked into the image (not the app's per-container env) so it rides the image republish rather than needing an app release, and applies to manual `docker run` use too. Upstream refs: anthropics/claude-code#70857, #72681.

## 5. Architecture

Three processes, per Electron convention:

```
┌────────────────────┐   IPC (contextBridge)   ┌────────────────────┐
│  Main (Node)       │ ◄─────────────────────► │  Renderer (React)  │
│  - dockerode       │                         │  Layout:           │
│  - safeStorage     │   exposes window.api    │   ┌─ top strip ─┐  │
│  - clipboard       │   via preload script    │   ├──┬───────┬──┤  │
│  - native Menu     │                         │   │S │ term  │ O│  │
│  - broker client   │                         │   ├──┴───────┴──┤  │
│  - better-sqlite3  │                         │   └─ bottom bar ┘  │
│  - JSONL watcher   │                         └────────────────────┘
└─────────┬──────────┘
          │
          │  Docker socket + Unix sockets to each container's broker
          ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Docker daemon                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │ runner ct 1                                                  │   │
│  │   tini (PID 1)                                               │   │
│  │     └─ broker  ◄────── unix socket ◄── host BrokerClient     │   │
│  │         ├─ claude PTY (session A)                            │   │
│  │         ├─ claude PTY (session B)                            │   │
│  │         └─ claude PTY (session C)                            │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │ runner ct 2  …                                               │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

binds (per container):
  <fleetRoot>/<id>       → /workspace   (private; only this container)
  <fleetRoot>/shared     → /shared      (shared; every container, rw)
  state/<id>/.claude     → /home/fleet/.claude
  state/<id>/claude.json → /home/fleet/.claude.json   (onboarding/trust seed)
  state/<id>/broker      → /run/broker   (broker socket directory)
```

A **remote workspace** runs `fleet-host` next to the broker in one container; desktops reach it over HTTP/WebSocket instead of a bind-mounted socket:

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ runner-host container (one pod)          │
│   tini → fleet-host-entry                │
│     ├─ broker ◄── unix socket ──┐        │
│     │    └─ claude PTYs         │        │      HTTP / WS, Bearer
│     └─ fleet-host (node) ───────┘  :8080 │ ◄──  GitHub token ── desktops
│          one upstream per session        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

`src/host/` is Electron-free and reuses `src/main/identity/`, the broker codec (`src/main/broker.ts`), `claudeArgs.ts` and `vaultScopes.ts`; `src/host/importGraph.test.ts` pins its graph to those modules plus `ws`, `yaml` and `ulid`.


The **broker** is a small Go daemon (//broker) shipped inside the runner image. It owns every claude PTY in the container and exposes them via a Unix-socket protocol. The host's Electron main process attaches via the bind-mounted socket directory rather than running `docker exec claude` directly. This is the foundation of "expert workspaces" (issue #18): PTYs outlive any individual host disconnect (app quit, app crash), so when the user pauses + closes the app + reopens + unpauses, every session reattaches to the same live `claude` process with its in-memory context (analyses, file watches, MCP server state) intact.

**Broker frame protocol.** The frame envelope is `[u32 totalLen BE][u8 type][payload]`, defined in `broker/internal/proto/proto.go` and mirrored as `FrameType` in `src/main/broker.ts`. PTY-session frames (host↔broker): `CREATE`/`CREATED`, `ATTACH`/`ATTACHED`, `DETACH`/`DETACHED`, `INPUT`, `OUTPUT`, `CLOSE`/`CLOSED`, `ENDED`, `RESIZE`, `LIST`/`SESSIONS`, `HISTORY`. Port-forward frames (added for dev-server preview):

| Frame | Hex | Dir | Payload | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `DIAL` | 0x14 | host→broker | JSON `{channel, port}` | dial `127.0.0.1:<port>` inside the container; bind to `channel` |
| `DIALED` | 0x15 | broker→host | JSON `{channel, ok, error?}` | dial result |
| `LISTPORTS` | 0x16 | host→broker | empty | request current LISTEN ports |
| `PORTS` | 0x17 | broker→host | JSON `{ports:[{port,pid?,cmdline?,session?},...]}` | listening-port snapshot with best-effort owner + session attribution |
| `KILLPORT` | 0x18 | host→broker | JSON `{port}` | terminate the command subtree serving `port` — the owner's session-attributed ancestor + all its descendants (SIGTERM, 2s grace, SIGKILL) |
| `KILLED` | 0x19 | broker→host | JSON `{ok, error?}` | kill outcome |

A broker channel is either a PTY session or a dialed TCP connection — disjoint kinds, host-allocated from the same monotonic counter so they never collide. Once `DIALED{ok:true}`, the channel reuses the existing `INPUT`/`OUTPUT`/`CLOSE`/`ENDED` frames as a raw byte relay — HTTP keep-alive and WebSocket/HMR pass through untouched. Port detection: `LISTPORTS` parses `/proc/net/tcp` and `/proc/net/tcp6`, keeps rows in state `0A` (LISTEN), extracts the local port, dedupes across both files, and excludes the broker's own TCP port (Windows) and the MCP port. Each port is attributed to its owning process via socket-inode → `/proc/*/fd` scan (`broker/internal/portscan`); `pid`/`cmdline` are omitted when unresolved (fd race, exotic mount). `cmdline` is capped at 120 runes. Attribution continues one step further: the owner's `/proc` ppid ancestry is walked (bounded at 32 hops, best-effort) against the session manager's live root-pid map, and the owning broker session id ships as `session` (omitted when unresolved: orphaned server, fd race, or hop limit reached). `KILLPORT` instructs the broker to kill a port's server: the broker re-resolves port→PID at kill time from the live socket — it never accepts a host-supplied PID — then kills the **whole command subtree**, not just the socket-owning leaf. It walks the owner's `/proc` ppid ancestry up to the child of the owning session's PTY root (the command the user actually launched) and SIGTERMs that process plus every descendant, escalating survivors to SIGKILL after a 2s grace (`portscan.KillTree`). Killing only the leaf let a supervisor (`nodemon`, `next dev`, npm, a shell respawn loop) relaunch the listener and reopen the port, which read as "kill does nothing" (#361-followup). When the owner can't be attributed to any live session root, the subtree is rooted at the owner itself — the walk never climbs past an unknown boundary toward the session shell.

Each broker session has **at most one live writer**. An `ATTACH` to a session that already holds a writer is rejected (`ATTACHED` with `OK:false`, error `session: already attached`) rather than silently replacing it — otherwise the displaced connection keeps believing it's attached while claude's OUTPUT flows to the newcomer, blinding the original. The host's normal re-attach `DETACH`es first, so it never trips this; the guard exists for a second connection on the socket (an external probe, or a future second window). Reconnect-after-disconnect still works: a dropped connection's deferred cleanup `DETACH`es its sessions, clearing the writer for the next attach. **One race remains** on the pause→quit→reopen→resume path: the pre-quit connection died while the container was paused, so the broker (frozen) hasn't run that connection's deferred cleanup yet. On `unpause` it reaps the dead connection and accepts the new ATTACH — but the host may reconnect in the millisecond before the reap completes and get rejected `already attached`. So the host **retries** an `already attached` ATTACH (`REATTACH_RETRIES`=12 × `REATTACH_RETRY_MS`=250ms ≈ 3s) in `attachPty`; a genuinely-live second writer keeps failing and the retry gives up.

The app uses Electron's default **native menu** (`Menu.setApplicationMenu`). The **Help** submenu is built in `src/main/appMenu.ts`: a disabled `claude-fleet v<version>` item (the at-a-glance answer to "what version am I running?"), then **Open Data Folder** (`app.getPath('userData')`) and **Open Log** (`getLogPath()` → `<userData>/error.log`), both opened WSL-aware via `openHostPath`. These are the primary escape hatches for users who need the running version, crash logs, or the state DB without knowing the path. The version string (`appVersionString()` in `src/main/appVersion.ts` — also served to workspaces via the `get_config` MCP tool) always carries the **build identity** (#298), because the bare semver can't distinguish a release build from main-N-commits-later, nor two builds of the same release: packaged builds report `0.11.0+abc1234` (semver build metadata; the short sha is **baked at build time** via an electron-vite `define` — `__BUILD_SHA__` in `electron.vite.config.ts`, `git rev-parse --short HEAD` with a `GITHUB_SHA` fallback — since git isn't available/meaningful at runtime in a packaged app); dev builds report `0.11.0-dev.abc1234` from a live `git rev-parse` (preferred over the baked sha, which goes stale as commits land; looked up once and cached). With no sha available (dev outside a git checkout) the plain version is shown. The bare sha is exposed as `appBuildSha()` and flows into `get_config`'s `app.sha` and the `app-start` error-log line.

**Main process** owns everything privileged:
- Docker daemon access via `dockerode` (default socket).
- Secret-vault encryption via Electron `safeStorage`.
- PTY session lifecycle: holds the duplex stream handle for each active `docker exec`, forwards data to the renderer over per-session IPC channels, forwards renderer input back to the stream, forwards resize events to Docker.
- JSONL transcript watching (`chokidar`) + SQLite persistence (`better-sqlite3`). The watcher tails every workspace's `<state>/<id>/.claude/projects/-workspace/*.jsonl` non-recursively and ingests new lines into the SQLite cache (see §7 *JSONL→SQLite cache*). Cost rollup (`src/main/pricing.ts`) groups events by `(model, service_tier)` and applies hardcoded Claude 4.x rates to derive USD; the rest of #2 (live push, slot consumers for chip/tab/context-bar) lands later.

**Preload** is a tightly scoped bridge. It uses `contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld('api', …)` to expose a typed `window.api` to the renderer. Window options: `contextIsolation: true`, `nodeIntegration: false`, `sandbox: false` (sandbox is off because the preload needs `ipcRenderer`).

**Renderer** is pure React. It has zero Node access. It can only do what `window.api` lets it do. Everything privileged flows through IPC.

The renderer layout is a 3-row × 3-col shell:
- **Top row** (`WorkspaceTabStrip`): app name, workspace chips (each with a per-workspace hue + status dot), a `+` button (adds a workspace — opens the modal to create a new one *or* resume a saved one; a bare glyph rather than "New workspace" copy because it isn't strictly new, #21), a Settings gear, the daemon status indicator (no pill chrome — a constant "Docker" label plus a dot that does the signaling: solid green when reachable, pulsing red with a hover tooltip when not, #23), `MOCK MODE` chip when active. The chip hue comes from the manifest's `color.hue` field when set, falling back to a name-hash of the same 14-hue preset palette so unset workspaces still get a stable distinct color. **The strip is the "warm" fleet: `running` + `paused`, plus `unreachable` workspaces whose `lastKnownState` was warm (#380)** — chips don't vanish on a daemon flap; they render dimmed/inert (no ⋮ menu, secondary line `unreachable · was running`) and selecting one shows an inert main-pane card. `stopped` + `deleted` + cold-`unreachable` are the "cold" fleet in the Saved list (`unreachable` badge, Resume/Recreate disabled while the daemon is down). The warm/cold rule lives in `fleetTemperature.ts` (`isWarm`/`isCold`). A workspace is in exactly one place. If the selected workspace leaves the warm set (e.g. stopped via its ⋮ menu), App re-selects the first warm workspace so the main pane never strands on a chip that's gone. Each chip carries a small secondary line below the workspace name driven by observability — `active 2m ago` when the session is fresh, `idle 1h ago` when it's been quiet > 5 min, or empty when no events have been ingested yet. The activity text reads off `summary.lastActiveAt` from the shared summary map described below. A running chip also shows a **busy indicator** — its status dot pulses and the secondary line reads `working…` while claude is actively working. Busy/idle is classified from claude's terminal-title (OSC 0) glyph: a quadrant-circle spinner `◐◑◒◓` (U+25D0–U+25D3, what claude emits today) or a legacy braille spinner (U+2800–U+28FF, older claude versions) means busy, `✳` (U+2733) means idle (`isBusyTitle` in `activityDetector.ts`). The glyph set is an upstream contract claude has silently changed before (#343 — braille → quadrant circles killed every busy indicator while tests stayed green), so an unrecognized non-ASCII leading glyph is treated as idle (fail-safe) **and logged once per session** (`activity-unknown-title-glyph` via `app.logError` / main `logError`) so the next upstream glyph change surfaces as a diagnostic instead of a dead indicator. The renderer sources the title from **xterm's own OSC parser** (`term.onTitleChange`, #283) rather than re-scanning the raw PTY stream, so classification is immune to how the stream was chunked. Because the flag is edge-sourced and claude writes the idle title exactly once (the spinner re-asserts every ~1s frame), a **level-triggered stale-busy watchdog** backstops it (`staleBusy.ts`): a session still flagged busy after `BUSY_SILENCE_TIMEOUT_MS` (10s) of total PTY silence has lost an idle edge upstream (ConPTY is documented dropping/coalescing title writes) and is cleared; a genuinely busy claude re-asserts within ~1s. This also guarantees a stale flag can't permanently wedge the idle-gated per-tab refresh queue (`readyToRefresh`). `TerminalSession` reports per-session flips up to `TerminalPane`, which emits the per-session busy *broker* id set via `onBusyIdsChange`; App resolves those to claude UUIDs and OR's them per workspace (`resolveBusyByWorkspace`) for the chip. **This glyph signal is only the low-latency fallback — the authoritative source is the peer-status reconciler (§*Authoritative session status* below, #286), which overrides the glyph per session so a stuck spinner self-heals (#283).** `TerminalPane` also emits the current **live** (non-ended) broker session id set via `onLiveIdsChange` whenever the set changes (mount, tab open/close, PTY end); App.tsx collects these per-workspace and resolves them to claude UUIDs via `observability.summaryForBrokerSession(…)` to build the `openSessions` map for the Sessions pane (see §8 *Browse & resume a past session*). Jump-to-open-tab is handled via `activateRequest` / `onActivateConsumed` (a `{ workspaceId, brokerSessionId, token }` triple; App routes it to the matching `TerminalPane` by `workspaceId`; mirrors `resumeRequest`/`onResumeConsumed`): when the Sessions pane clicks an open row, App sets `activateRequest`; `TerminalPane` consumes it (token-guarded) and calls `setActiveId(brokerSessionId)`. The **same busy pulse appears on every "session chip"**: the active terminal **session tab's** dot and each matching row in the left-rail **Sessions list**, both driven by the **peer-reconciled** busy set (`resolveBusyClaudeIds`, keyed by claude session UUID) rather than the raw glyph, so all three busy indicators — workspace chip, Sessions row, session-tab dot — agree for the same session state. For the Sessions list — keyed by claude session UUID, not broker id — App resolves the busy broker ids to claude UUIDs via `observability.summaryForBrokerSession(...).sessionId` (re-resolving on observability pushes, since the broker→claude mapping is learned as transcripts ingest) and passes the resulting peer-reconciled `busySessionIds` set down, so only the genuinely-running session's row pulses. The **session-tab dot receives the same set** (App passes `busySessionIds` into `TerminalPane`) and resolves it per tab with `resolveTabBusy(claudeId, brokerId, busySessionIds, glyphBusyIds)`: once the tab's broker→claude mapping is known the reconciled set governs (so a peer-idle session clears a stuck-busy tab dot just as it clears the chip), and the raw glyph (`busyIds`) is the fallback only while that mapping is unresolved — the same "glyph governs only sessions the peer hasn't reported yet" precedence `resolveBusyByWorkspace` uses for the chip (#371; previously the tab dot read `TerminalPane`'s local glyph directly and could silently disagree, since a missed OSC busy edge left it dark and the stale-busy watchdog only *clears* a flag, never sets one). **All busy indicators blink in lockstep**: each pulse element aligns its CSS `animation-delay` to a shared wall-clock phase (`blinkSync.ts` — `useBlinkSync`/`blinkDelayMs`), so an indicator that mounts mid-cycle (e.g. a Sessions row that remounts on a list refresh) snaps to the same phase as one that has been pulsing for a while, rather than drifting. (The title glyph itself can't tell idle from "needs your input" — claude renders AskUserQuestion/permission prompts with no distinct glyph and doesn't write them to the JSONL while pending; that gap is now filled by the peer-status reconciler below, not the glyph. See §11.)

A running chip also surfaces a **waiting indicator** — a violet (`--wait`) status dot that **reuses the busy pulse** plus a `?` glyph and a `needs input` secondary line — when a session is blocked on an `AskUserQuestion` prompt. **Waiting wins over busy**: if a session is in the waiting set, its chip/tab dot shows violet rather than the busy pulse. The same violet state appears on the session-tab dot and the Sessions-list row. Unlike busy/idle (PTY title glyph), waiting is sourced from a runner hook: a `PreToolUse[AskUserQuestion]` hook reports `waiting=true`; `PostToolUse[AskUserQuestion]`, `Stop`, and `UserPromptSubmit` report `waiting=false`, all via the `signal_input_wait` MCP tool (§6). Main keeps a per-workspace `Set<string>` of waiting claude session UUIDs and pushes `inputwait:update` `{ workspaceId, waitingSessionIds }` to renderers on every change; the set is cleared when the workspace stops, pauses, or is removed. App keys the Sessions list directly by claude UUID and resolves the session tab via `summaryForBrokerSession`. The hook is **container-only** and covers **`AskUserQuestion` only** — but it is no longer the sole waiting source: the peer-status reconciler below extends waiting to **all backends and all prompt kinds** (permission prompts included) and authoritatively clears a stale hook entry, so the renderer unions the two (`resolveWaitingClaudeIds` / `resolveWaitingByWorkspace`) with peer-status winning.

**Authoritative session status (peer-status reconciler, #286).** Claude writes an authoritative per-process status file, `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` = `{pid, sessionId, cwd, status: busy|idle|waiting, waitingFor?, statusUpdatedAt}`. This is the ground truth the title glyph only approximates — the glyph can't distinguish idle from waiting, and any signal parsed from PTY output can be lost upstream (the #283 stale-busy gap, where this file was correct the whole time the UI was stuck). These files are **host-reachable for every backend**, so a single host-side watcher covers everything with **no broker-protocol change**: a container's `~/.claude` *is* the bind-mounted `<state>/<id>/.claude` (§*Docker container shape*), so its `sessions/` dir shows up on the host; a native local workspace uses the host's real `~/.claude/sessions/`; a WSL workspace's dir is read over the `\\wsl.localhost` 9P share (polled, like transcripts). `PeerStatusWatcher` (`peerStatusWatcher.ts`, a native watcher plus a polled instance, mirroring `jsonlWatcher`: `RecursiveDirWatcher` at depth 0 on Windows/macOS, chokidar on Linux, see §4 *File watcher*) tails these dirs, parses each file (`peerStatus.ts`), and reduces to one status per claude session id — newest `statusUpdatedAt` wins, so a stale pid file left by a resumed session can't override the live one (`reducePeerStatuses`). Main pushes the flat snapshot to every renderer as `sessionstatus:update` `{ statuses: [{claudeSessionId, status, waitingFor?}] }` (flat because claude session ids are globally unique — the renderer attributes them to workspaces via its own broker→claude `mappings`). **Precedence (authoritative override):** where the peer knows a session, its verdict wins (busy/idle/waiting); the glyph and the AskUserQuestion hook govern only sessions the peer hasn't reported yet. The merge is pure and unit-tested (`sessionStatusMerge.ts`); a peer-idle session clears a stuck busy chip and a stale hook-waiting entry alike. Registered/torn down alongside the transcript watcher (startup, `workspace:create`, `workspace:remove`); the shared host `~/.claude/sessions/` used by native local workspaces stays registered across a single workspace removal (other local workspaces and the user's own claude sessions share it — foreign session ids in the snapshot are harmless, the renderer only uses ids it has mapped).
- **Centralized observability distribution.** `App.tsx` owns a single `summaries: Record<workspaceId, WorkspaceSummary | null>` map (keyed by ULID) and distributes it to the chip strip, observability pane, and terminal-pane context bar via props. The map is filled two ways:
  1. **Live push.** The main-process `JsonlWatcher` emits `'ingest'` after every batch that genuinely inserts ≥1 new event (compaction re-reads that hit dedup_key are suppressed). `ipc.ts` subscribes, computes the workspace summary, and sends `observability:summary` to every BrowserWindow with `{ workspaceId, summary }`. The renderer's `window.api.observability.onSummary(cb)` (registered in App.tsx) updates the map immediately — chip relative-time, USD total, and context-bar fill refresh in <100ms of the JSONL flush.
  2. **30s safety poll.** App.tsx also re-fetches every workspace's summary every 30s. This backs up the push for any lost event and forces a re-render so the chip's `Date.now() - lastActiveAt` text rolls forward ("active 2m ago" → "active 12m ago") even when no new ingests are happening. It's 15× less frequent than the previous unconditional 2s poll because push handles the hot path.
- **Body row** (3 columns):
  - **Sessions pane** (left, ~280px): placeholder until #3 lands the JSONL-backed sessions table.
  - **Main pane** (center, fluid): header with selected workspace's name/status and `Close…` button, plus the xterm `TerminalPane` (or empty/first-run/disconnected states). The **first-run state** (shown whenever the live fleet is empty, `App.FirstRun`) doubles as the product's pitch rather than a bare "no workspaces" message: a centered hero (headline + lede + the primary *Launch your first workspace* CTA), a responsive grid of feature cards naming the distinctive capabilities and the value each buys (parallel fleet, per-workspace Docker isolation, pause/resume expert workspaces, live cost/token/tool observability, the cross-workspace Committee, drag-and-drop ingestion), and a footer strip name-dropping secondary features (loadouts, session history/resume, keychain secrets, fleet-state MCP). It scrolls within `.main-body`; styling lives under `.landing*` / `.feature-card` in `styles.css` and leans on the `--ok` accent. At the top of the terminal area, a **context bar** carries the workspace's hue track + a width-driven fill — `--pct` set inline by `TerminalPane` from `summary.lastTurnContextTokens / summary.contextWindowTokens × 100`. The effective context window comes from `src/main/contextWindow.ts`: 200K per Claude 4.x family by default, 1M when the model id carries the `[1m]` marker (e.g. `claude-opus-4-7[1m]`), and a heuristic 1M auto-upgrade when any observed turn in the session has already crossed 200K (catches the 1M beta header case, since that flag doesn't show up in the model string Claude Code writes to JSONL). Falls back to a full identity band (100%) when no observability data is available, so a fresh workspace still reads visually correct. Tooltip on the band shows `tokens / limit (pct%)`. A subtle vertical tick at 80% (`.terminal-accent-band::after`) marks the compaction threshold — claude auto-compacts around there, so the tick is the heads-up that the next turn might trigger one.
  - **Observability pane** (right, ~320px): just under the rail header sits the **plan-usage bar** (`UsageBudgetBar`) — a fleet-wide "tokens left" gauge for the current rolling window (default 5h), shown regardless of scope or selection because the plan limit is account-wide, not per-workspace (hidden entirely — poll included — when `uiPrefs.showBudgetBar` is off, §*Settings*). Numerator = `usage:rollingSpend` (all four token types summed across the fleet where `ts ≥ now − window`, polled every 15s); denominator = the configured `usageBudget.allowanceTokens` (§*Settings*). Framed as **percent remaining** ("NN% left" — the user's framing), with a depleting fill tinted warn ≤25% / danger ≤10% left and a `spent / allowance` subline; degrades to a spend-only readout when the allowance is 0. Below it, a **scope toggle** switches between *This workspace* and *Fleet · N*. **Fleet scope** is a pure-renderer aggregate built from the shared per-workspace `summaries` map (no extra IPC): total USD across all live workspaces, a stacked-share bar (one hue segment per workspace, `flex` = its `usd`), and per-workspace rows (hue dot + name + state + cost). Below the per-workspace rows, a **Serving** section lists all probe-passed ports across the fleet — one row per port: workspace hue-dot + name, port number, label (declared ports; cmdline in the tooltip) or cmdline when known, session chip after that (same info-tinted pill, same click-to-focus behavior as workspace scope); uptime lives in the workspace-name tooltip. Hidden when no workspace has serving ports. **Workspace scope** is the default — a live view of the **active terminal tab**'s Claude session in the selected workspace. Shows session title (from `ai-title` event or first-user-message head), latest model, last-activity relative time, event count, and a **session graph** with a **cost ⇄ tokens metric toggle** (`$`/`tok` segmented control; preference persisted to `localStorage` under `obsGraphMetric`, default cost): in **cost** mode the headline is the USD total and the per-turn **sparkline** plots `costSeries`; in **tokens** mode the headline is total session tokens (input+output+cache) and the sparkline plots `tokenSeries` (per-turn total tokens — same per-turn definition as `costSeries`, so the two graphs line up bar-for-bar). Accent bars with recent turns brighter; hidden until ≥2 non-zero turns. Then token totals (input / cache-create / cache-read / output), recent tool calls (name + input summary + duration + ok/error status, from `recentToolCalls`), then the **Serving** section (rendered between Recent tools and the Workspace card, absent when empty) — one row per HTTP-serving port, in order: `:port · label (declared ports; cmdline in the tooltip) or cmdline when known · [session chip] · uptime · ↗ · ✕`; session chip is an info-tinted pill showing the owning tab's name — renders when the broker attributed the port to a live session AND that session id is resolvable to a name from `sessions:read` (via `useServingSessionNames`); clicking it calls `setSelectedId` + `setActivateRequest` to switch workspace and focus the tab via the `activateRequest` jump path; absent otherwise; ↗ opens preview (same path as the detection toast); ✕ is always visible — two-step inline confirm with a 3s auto-revert; a pre-KILLPORT broker times out and the handler returns `{ ok: false, error: 'runner image too old — recreate the workspace to enable kill' }`, which the renderer toasts verbatim; and a **Context · N terminals** section — one fill bar per session tab in the workspace (App reads `sessions.json` and fetches `summaryForBrokerSession` per tab), tinted warn ≥75% / danger ≥90% with an 80% compaction tick and the active tab dotted/bold. Empty state when the focused tab has no per-tab data yet. Below the session data, a **Workspace** block (rendered whenever a workspace is selected, even before any events) shows two clickable folder rows plus the runner image and resource limits in a bordered card: **private** (`<fleetRoot>/<id>` mounted at /workspace, visible only to that container) and **shared** (`<fleetRoot>/shared` mounted into every container). For a container workspace, one additional `mount` row follows per entry in `manifest.mounts` — container path (`m.containerPath`) plus an `ro` tag when `m.readOnly` — each a button titled `Open <hostPath>` (`… (read-only)` suffix when applicable). Each row is a button that reveals the host folder in the OS file manager via `fs:openPath`. The shared path comes from `config:get` (fetched once in App.tsx). Per-tab resolution comes from the `broker_sessions` mapping table (§11 *Per-tab mapping*). `TerminalPane` bubbles its active tab id up via `onActiveTabChange`; App.tsx fetches `summaryForBrokerSession(workspaceId, activeTabId)` for the selected workspace and re-fetches on every push for that workspace. **No workspace fallback** — when the per-tab fetch returns null the pane shows its empty state directly, regardless of how the tab was added. The fallback was tried (loaded-from-inventory tabs → workspace summary, fresh tabs → empty) but produced two user-visible bugs: clicking `+` showed the previous tab's data via the fallback, and switching between two unmapped tabs showed the same workspace-summary content on both ("doesn't update"). Per-tab semantics are now honest: each tab shows its own data or empty, never inherits from a sibling tab. The pane header carries a **collapse toggle** (`›`) that minimizes the rail to a thin 28px reopen strip (the `.app-body` grid's third column shrinks `320px`→`28px`); the strip is one tall click target whose `‹` button restores it. State is held in `App.tsx` (`obsCollapsed`) and persisted to `localStorage` under `obsRailCollapsed` — a pure UI preference, so it deliberately uses `localStorage` rather than the main-side `config.json`. The rail stays mounted while collapsed only in the sense that React re-renders it as the strip; the heavy summary subtree is not rendered, but the shared `summaries` map in App keeps polling so reopening is instant.
- **Bottom row** (`BottomBar`): static hint bar with key bindings and degraded-vault notice when applicable.

Modals owned by `App`:
- All modals render inside the shared `ModalBackdrop` component, which dismisses only when a left-button press **starts and ends on the backdrop element itself** (tracked via mousedown/mouseup targets, pure logic in `backdropDismiss.ts`) — a plain `onClick={onClose}` backdrop would also close on a text-selection drag that releases over the backdrop, because the browser fires `click` on the common ancestor of the down/up targets. Passing `onClose={undefined}` disables backdrop dismissal (used while a modal is `busy`).
- `WorkspaceModal` — tabbed shell with Saved + New tabs (underlined-tab style). Body of each tab uses `WorkspaceForm` — the field-level form component owns state, validation, and footer; New tab renders it in `mode='create'`, expanded Saved-tab rows render it in `mode='edit'`. Saved-row expanded footer is `Delete · Cancel · Clone · Resume`; New-tab footer is `Cancel · Create & start`.
- `EditWorkspaceModal` — single-purpose modal for editing a *live* workspace. Opened from the chip ⋮ menu Edit entry. Wraps `WorkspaceForm` in `mode='edit'`. On Save, calls `workspace:writeManifest` + diffs pre/post specs; container-level changes flip the restart-to-apply banner in the workspace's TerminalPane.
- `CloseWorkspaceModal` — Stop / Pause / Stop & remove (keeps state dir).
- `DeleteWorkspaceModal` — Permanent purge confirmation: stop + `workspace:remove(containerId, { deleteState: true })` + `vault:deleteAllForWorkspace(id)`.
