Adding `reschedule`, lowering its `min`, or dropping every schedule trigger while it is on needs you (#437), and approval cards flag "sets its own next run (no sooner than <min>)". Anything that widens reach (new prompt target, live↔fresh, extra `allowedTools`, `ensureRunning`, new event triggers, wider `allowFrom`, faster than every 5 min, adding a schedule) or is a new automation needs you. Each item carries readable `reasons` and capability `flags` ("prompts ops-oncall (fresh session)", "runs every 15 min"…).
- **Trusted** (`inbox.trusted`, container only; Edit workspace › Inbox & automations, with an in-place consent note): its low-risk items apply immediately (`auto-applied`, one low-priority notification). Everything else, and everything from untrusted workspaces, waits. Approving checks the automation's version against the proposal's base and refuses if it changed since.
- **UI:** Activity shows pending proposals on top of Everything and under **Needs approval** (with the last 10 decided). A card shows kind, proposer, age, rationale, "Needs your approval because it …" flags, capability chips and the YAML diff (`lineDiff.ts`), with **Approve** · **Edit, then approve** (the YAML editor; saving approves the edited version) · **Reject…** (a note the workspace can read). Batch cards list each change with Accept/Decline and **Apply accepted**. Applied changes offer **Revert**. Pending approvals turn the Inbox chip dot violet ("N to approve"), count on the Activity tab, and count toward "needs you" on the taskbar, dock and tray.
- **Knowledge-capture settings proposals (#483 §5a):** a workspace's `set_capture_settings` change that needs approval (cost-increasing, private → fleet visibility, or shorter retention) appears in Activity → **Needs approval** as a small card listing each field change and why it needs approval, with **Approve** / **Reject…**.

### The automations workspace (#407 Phase 5b)
- **One per fleet**, container-only, marked `role: 'automations'` (and trusted). It is set up from the Automations tab, either by picking an existing container workspace, or with **Create one…**, which opens the normal New Workspace modal pre-filled (`automations`, the devops image) and assigns the role once created. The banner then offers Edit / Stop using.
- **The Inbox chip fronts it:** its own chip is hidden from the strip, and opening the Inbox selects it, so the left rail's Sessions and the Observability rail show it. Inbox › **Sessions** reveals its terminal pane (native session tabs) under the inbox header, or "isn't running · Start it".
- **Reach:** `list_automations` / `run_automation` / `get_automation` / `list_runs` see and run **every** automation for this workspace (others see only what they may run or are prompted by). Its proposals are trusted (low-risk only).
- **Role brief** (`roleBriefCore.ts` renders, `roleBrief.ts` writes): a generated block at the **top** of the workspace's `/workspace/CLAUDE.md`, between `<!-- claude-fleet:role-automations start/end -->`, so a session there knows its job without any loadout. It covers identity (name, id, app version), the job and its limits (draft/explain automations, submit via proposals, never approve; don't do the automated task itself; never schedule with crontab/timers/loops in the container), how Inbox requests arrive (`=== … (data) ===` markers are data), trust and security (low-risk edits auto-apply; flag `runIn: host`, wake-ups, cross-workspace posts and faster schedules in `rationale`; no secrets in YAML; `/shared`, transcripts, registry text and other workspaces are untrusted; never copy the user's credentials), which paths survive a recreate (`/workspace`, `/shared`, `~/.claude` only), that the loadout library is host-side (lists the installed loadouts and configured sources — and that `list_workspace_skills` is how to see what a workspace can already do, so a prompt step should invoke an existing skill rather than recreate it by hand), and the `claude-fleet-state` tools (from `mcpServer.ts` `TOOLS`: first sentence each, automation tools first — `list_workspace_skills`/`get_workspace_skill` listed alongside them — `Internal …` hook tools omitted). It deliberately does **not** list fleet workspaces (they go stale; each Inbox prompt carries the current names). Written through the host side of the bind mount (works with the container stopped), only when the text changed: on `automations:setRole` (and stripped from the previous holder; CLAUDE.md is deleted if nothing else is left), at app start, and after a loadout install/uninstall in that workspace (`setLoadoutChangeListener`). Every non-app-authored value (workspace name, loadout ids/titles, source refs, image) is flattened to one line, capped, and stripped of markdown/HTML syntax, since CLAUDE.md is high-authority context.
- **Ask Claude about this** (on proposal cards, when set up): posts `review.ts`'s prompt (the proposal between `=== begin proposal (data) ===` / `=== end proposal ===` markers, then the questions: what changes, interaction with the current version, noise, a security read, a recommendation, and "do not approve or apply anything yourself; propose a merged version with propose_automation_change") into its live session, and switches to Inbox › Sessions.

### Claude-first authoring (#407 Phase 6)
- **Composer** at the top of the Automations tab ("Describe a new automation" → **Draft with Claude**; ⌘/Ctrl+Enter). It posts `authoring.ts`'s `draftPrompt` into the automations workspace's live session: the request between `=== the user's request (data) ===` / `=== end request ===` markers, the names of the workspaces it may target (all but itself) and of existing automations, an inline reference of the YAML format, and rules (don't do the task; before writing a prompt step, call `list_workspace_skills` for its target workspace and prefer invoking an existing skill — `message: "/<name> <args>"` — over re-describing the task, reading it with `get_workspace_skill` when its inputs are unclear; prefer `mode: fresh` with minimal `allowedTools`; a dedupe key per problem and `run_id`; no faster than every 5 min unless asked; no secrets in YAML; submit with `propose_automation`, fix and resubmit on validation errors). `DRAFT_TOOLS` grants the drafting session `list_workspace_skills`/`get_workspace_skill` alongside the proposal/automation tools, read-only. The draft returns as an ordinary **new-automation proposal** in Activity. **Write YAML…** opens the YAML editor from the starter template instead. Without an automations workspace the composer is disabled with a hint. The skills-discovery rule lives in the shared `RULES` list used by the draft, change and refine prompts alike.
- **Ask Claude to change …** (detail pane of an automation) posts `changePrompt`: the current YAML and the change request, each delimited as data; the answer is a `propose_automation_change` (a diff card).
- **Ask for changes…** (pending single-item proposal cards whose proposer *is* the automations workspace) posts `refinePrompt` with the current draft; it keeps the name, so the new proposal supersedes the card. Proposals from other workspaces don't offer it (their proposer couldn't supersede them).
- Each send shows "Sent to …" with **Watch** (Inbox › Sessions). Requests are capped at 8000 characters.

## 9. Security model

- **Secret env-var values stay out of the renderer except at write time and on explicit reveal.** Per-workspace secrets are persisted via `vault:setSecret(workspaceId, key, value)`. After that, the renderer holds only the *list* of secret key names (via `vault:listKeys`); the main process resolves values directly from the safeStorage vault when constructing the container env. Two exceptions, both in `WorkspaceForm`'s env editor: (1) the renderer briefly holds the value the user just typed before it ships to `vault:setSecret` — there is no way around that; (2) the user clicks a saved secret's eye button, which fetches that one value via `vault:getSecret` and holds it only while shown — dropped from state on hide, after 30 s, on any edit to the row, or when the form unmounts. Nothing reads a saved value without that click.
- **Loadout-source tokens are write-only from the renderer and bound to their host (#444).**
  - **Where it lives:** a private source's token lives only in the vault (`loadout-source:<base>`) and in main-process memory during a request.
  - **Where it never goes:** a container, `sources.json`, a log line or an error message (errors name the source).
  - **Renderer access:** the renderer can submit a token (`loadouts:addSource`, `loadouts:setSourceCredential`) but has no read path. The generic `vault:*` IPC refuses `loadout-source:` scopes and fails closed on a non-string scope or key, since a structured-cloned array or `String` object would otherwise coerce to the real scope string.
  - **Reserved workspace ids:** a workspace id doubles as its own vault scope, so `workspace:create` and `workspace:writeManifest` refuse a renderer-supplied id containing `:` or starting with a reserved vault prefix (`loadout-source:`, `endpoint:`, `identity:`); `vault.resolveEnv` refuses a reserved scope as a backstop.
  - **Where it is sent:** only to the source's registry host, and to its advertised token realm when that realm is `https:` on the same host or a known pairing (`ociCore.KNOWN_REALM_HOSTS`). A realm on any other host is asked anonymously, so a hostile `WWW-Authenticate` can't redirect credentials. The GHCR blob CDN redirect is handled by relying on `fetch` (undici) dropping `Authorization` on a cross-origin redirect, never by inspecting the redirect target.
  - **Error sanitisation:** a registry auth failure's message carries only the source base and an HTTP status (`RegistryAuthError`); transport-level throws (DNS failures, aborts, a malformed-header rejection from undici) are caught and re-thrown naming only the stage and host, never the original message or `cause` — the one place a raw token could otherwise leak into a toast or log.
  - **Local workspaces never inherit a source token:** `FLEET_LOADOUT_TOKEN` and any `FLEET_LOADOUT_TOKEN_*` env var are stripped from a local (non-container) workspace's spawn env — the main process resolves and uses them to pull loadouts, but a workspace's own `claude` process must not see them.
- **Postgres store credentials stay in main.**
  - **Renderer:** `store:setChoice` can write only `sqlite` or the managed form, never a connection or credential; `store` isn't in `config:get`, and the desktop refuses plaintext secrets in it (§6 *Settings*). External credentials come from env vars or `aws-rds-iam`.
  - **Managed container:** the `fleet_app` password lives in the vault (`store:managed`, host-only) and reaches Postgres only as a SCRAM verifier inside a `docker exec` env var; no container or exec inspect carries the plaintext. The superuser has no password and `pg_hba` rejects it over TCP. The port is bound to `127.0.0.1`. Other local users reaching the port still need `fleet_app`'s password.
  - **SCRAM only without TLS** (`store/pgScramOnly.ts`): every pool and client with `ssl` off (the managed store, external `disable` stores, fleet-host, `fleet-host bootstrap`'s admin login, the `query` role pools) uses `ScramOnlyClient`, which drops the connection when the server asks for a cleartext or md5 password, so a process squatting the port can't collect one. An external non-TLS server whose `pg_hba` uses `md5` or `password` is therefore refused. `verify-full` pools allow cleartext (RDS IAM sends its token that way over TLS). Every one of these clients, TLS or not, extends `ClosingClient`, which destroys the socket when a login fails: pg leaves it open (e.g. after a password provider throws mid-SCRAM), and the server backend would wait in authentication until `authentication_timeout`, stalling every `DROP DATABASE` on the cluster meanwhile.
  - **Local workspaces:** `storeSecretEnvNames` lists the store's credential env vars: the `env` connection-string variable, an `env` password's variable, and the libpq credential vars `PGPASSWORD`, `PGPASSFILE`, `PGSERVICE`, `PGSERVICEFILE`, `PGSSLKEY` and `PGSSLCERT`. On the Postgres store, these are stripped (case-insensitively) from a local workspace's spawn env and from the host `claude` PATH probe (the union of the configured store's list and the running store's, so a switch pending restart still scrubs; the managed store's list is the libpq vars, and its password is never in an env var the app sets), because a workspace holding them could read every workspace's rows and bypass MCP isolation. When `config.json` is unreadable, the libpq vars are stripped anyway. **This scrub is defense in depth, not a boundary:** a local workspace's `claude` runs as the host user (*Local-workspace launcher trust*, below) and so can still read `~/.pgpass`, `~/.aws` and any ambient `AWS_*` variable the stripped list doesn't name — the same pre-existing local-workspace trust caveat as everywhere else in this section.
  - **No leaks:** store errors, log lines and the startup dialog never carry a password, token or connection string. Config errors name keys only, and pg's errors carry host, port and user.
- **Postgres TLS can't be downgraded by config.**
  - **Modes:** `disable` and `verify-full` are the only modes: CA from `caFile`, plus Node's hostname check. Nothing sets `rejectUnauthorized: false`, and the verify-full options object is frozen.
  - **Connection strings:** `sslmode` must be `disable` or `verify-full`, and every other `ssl*` parameter and `uselibpqcompat` are refused. `pgPoolConfig` always sets `ssl` explicitly, so pg never consults `PGSSLMODE`.
  - **Default:** a connection string without `sslmode` is `verify-full`, unless its effective host is a Unix socket or loopback (`localhost`, `127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`). The effective host is pg's own fallback: `?host=`, then the URL host, then `PGHOST`, then `localhost`. A configured `caFile` means `verify-full` even on loopback, and `caFile` with `disable` is refused.
  - **`aws-rds-iam`** requires `verify-full`, enforced both at parse and in `pgPoolConfig`.
- **MCP `query` on Postgres is isolated by role and row-level security** (`store/pgQueryIsolation.ts`).
  - **Roles:**
    - Each caller workspace reads as its own LOGIN NOINHERIT role, `<schema>_mcp_<sha256(id)[:16]>`.
    - That role is a member of `<schema>_mcp_reader`. The reader has schema `USAGE`, `EXECUTE` on `mcp_scope()` and `mcp_row_too_large()`, and `SELECT` on the eight `query` tables. On `events` it gets only the snapshot columns: no `raw_jsonl`, no `dedup_key`.
    - The role's NOLOGIN `_raw` sibling adds `raw_jsonl` (via `<schema>_mcp_raw`). It is granted `WITH INHERIT FALSE, SET TRUE`; the call does `SET LOCAL ROLE` to it when `include_raw`.
    - `include_raw` sets the default projection; it is not a privilege boundary. A caller can `set_config('role', '<caller>_raw', true)` itself and read `raw_jsonl`, and RLS still limits it to its own scope's rows.
    - `ensureIsolation` revokes every table privilege from the reader and raw roles before granting, so a stale grant can't survive a restart.
    - `ensureIsolation` stores a fingerprint (a hash of its DDL plus a digest of the RLS flags, table and column ACLs, policies, functions and shared roles) as the comment on `mcp_grants`. When the catalog still matches, it runs no DDL, so a normal start takes no ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock. Otherwise the DDL runs with `lock_timeout` 3 s and `statement_timeout` 30 s; a lock timeout leaves `query` unavailable with the reason, and boot continues.
    - A process mints each role's password at first use and sets it as a SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier. Nothing stores it.
  - **Rows:**
    - RLS on the eight tables admits `workspace_id IN mcp_scope()`; `perf_events` also admits NULL.
    - Policies target only the reader role, so any other non-owner role sees no rows. RLS stays enabled on these eight tables, so this includes any role an operator adds for reporting or a BI tool: it needs the store role, a BYPASSRLS role, or its own policy.
    - `mcp_scope()` is `SECURITY DEFINER` with `search_path = pg_catalog`, so a function shadowed on the caller's path can't hijack it. It returns the `mcp_grants` rows for `session_user`.
    - The caller's SQL can reach only its own roles with `SET ROLE` or `set_config('role')`, and can't change `session_user`.
    - `mcp_grants` is rewritten from the caller's allowed set before every call, under a per-caller lock, so a revocation applies to the next call.
    - The store role owns the tables without `FORCE`, so its own queries bypass RLS.
  - **Statement:**
    - The caller's SQL becomes the inner subquery of one `DECLARE … CURSOR` that returns each row as size-checked `row_to_json` text (§11 *`query` on the Postgres store: limits*).
    - The cursor accepts only SELECT, VALUES or WITH (no data-modifying WITH), sent over the extended protocol, which refuses a second statement.
    - It runs in a REPEATABLE READ transaction set READ ONLY, with `statement_timeout` 5 s, `lock_timeout` 2 s (each re-set before every FETCH, §11 *`query` on the Postgres store: limits*) and `TimeZone` UTC. Eight temp views shadow the tables first, so `SELECT * FROM events` has no `raw_jsonl`.
    - Every call ends with `ROLLBACK` and `DISCARD ALL`, because advisory locks and `LISTEN` survive a rollback.
    - Tested and refused:
      - reading another workspace's rows by join, subquery, `query_to_xml` or a schema-qualified table;
      - `set_config('role'|'session_authorization')`;
      - COPY, PREPARE/EXECUTE and LISTEN;
      - writes and temp objects (including via `query_to_xml` and `lo_*`);
      - server files (`pg_read_file`, `pg_ls_dir`, `lo_import`);
      - dblink and postgres_fdw;
      - session state carried across calls;
      - a `pg_notify` escaping the rollback.
  - **Visible by design:** catalog metadata (§11 *`query` on the Postgres store: limits*, accepted risks). `pg_stats` hides RLS tables' column values.
  - **Needs:** Postgres 16 or later and a store role with `CREATEROLE` (on PG 16 it administers only roles it created). Without them `query` reports why and every other tool works. `removeWorkspaceSocket` drops a removed workspace's roles and grants (`forgetCaller`).
  - **On fleet-host** the same module isolates `query` for the hosted workspace (§7 *Host MCP*) in `preprovisioned` mode: no store-role `CREATEROLE`, no per-caller role. `fleet-host bootstrap` (admin credentials) creates the reader/raw roles and the one IAM query login and revokes any other membership; each call re-checks that the login reaches no other role, so a later grant (to the app role, say) disables `query` instead of widening it.
- **The GitHub sign-in token stays in main (remote-workspaces (b)).**
  - **Where it lives:** the vault scope `identity:github` (or main-process memory on a degraded vault).
  - **Where it never goes:** the renderer (no `identity:*` response carries it, and `vault:*` refuses the scope), a container, a log line or an error message. `IdentityError` and `DeviceFlowError` messages reach the UI — both are token-free; non-identity errors inside poll/refresh are replaced by a generic message. The device code also stays in main.
  - **Where it is sent:** only `https://api.github.com` (profile and teams; pagination `Link`s off that host aren't followed) and, as a GHCR credential, private `ghcr.io` loadout sources (never the public default). The device flow talks only to `https://github.com`. A `verification_uri` or SSO URL that isn't `https://github.com/…` is replaced or dropped. Every GitHub request uses `redirect: 'error'`.
  - **Scopes:** `read:org read:packages`, the minimum. Public profile fields need no scope, and `read:user` isn't requested.
  - **Races:** sign-out wins over an in-flight refresh or sign-in, including one whose vault write is under way. A 401 on refresh clears only the token it probed, so revoking an old token can't undo a newer sign-in.
- **Verified identities are cached for 5 minutes** (`withVerifyCache`, keyed by the token's SHA-256; failures aren't cached). Revoking a token, removing someone from a team, or moving an App installation out of its org takes effect within that window.
- **Team lookups fail closed.** A `/user/teams` 403 is read as "no teams" only when it carries an SSO hint or an `X-OAuth-Scopes` header lacking `read:org`; a 403 with no `X-OAuth-Scopes` header (e.g. a fine-grained token) can't be verified and throws `upstream`.
- **Machine tokens are org-bound.** A `machines` rule's tier applies only to an installation whose every repository is owned by the rule's org (`org` or `identity.org`), with at least one repository. An installation with more than 1000 repositories (10 pages) or a pagination link off `api.github.com` is refused. GitHub-operated shared Apps (`github-actions` 15368, `dependabot` 29110) are rejected at config parse: use a dedicated org-owned App.
- **The GitHub token reaches a remote host only with consent (remote-workspaces (c)).**
  - **Where it is sent:** `RemoteClient` (main) attaches it as a Bearer only to the canonical URL the client was built for — the URL is normalised and copied at construction; `fetch` uses `redirect: 'error'` and ws `followRedirects: false` (a 3xx upgrade is reported, not followed). A token outside the bearer charset is refused before sending. `bearerToken()` is wired only in `ipc.ts`; preload, `identityIpc` and `remoteIpc` never see it, and no `remote:*` reply carries it.
  - **Consent is per origin** (`URL.origin`, punycode, so look-alikes show as `xn--`). Before the first token-bearing call to an origin with no `kind: 'remote'` bookmark, main shows a native dialog ("Send your GitHub sign-in to <origin>?", Cancel default); a non-loopback `http:` origin's dialog adds the plain-http warning. An approval covers that origin for 2 min (probe → add); concurrent asks for one origin share one dialog. A declined origin is remembered for the app session (answered `declined` at once). At most one dialog per 10 s across origins and kinds, and none while another is open; extra asks return `declined` without being remembered. Moving a bookmark (`sameHostNewAddress`) needs its own native confirm naming both URLs; a declined `(from, to)` is remembered, and it may follow the send-token dialog that just approved its destination without waiting. Because trust is per origin, a host must not share an origin with untrusted apps (path-routed ingresses).
  - **Trust is inferred from manifests** under `<userData>/state`. A container can't write there, but a local (non-container) workspace's claude runs as the user and can: it could plant a `kind: 'remote'` bookmark and so skip the dialog for its origin. Same caveat as every other local-workspace boundary.
  - **Where it never goes:** errors and the renderer. Host text (HTTP bodies, ATTACHED/CREATED errors) is redacted (exact token, then `redactSecrets`), stripped of control and Unicode format (`\p{Cf}`, bidi) characters and capped at 200 chars; a malformed attach reply reports a fixed "malformed host reply", since `JSON.parse` quotes the host's bytes; fetch errors report only `cause.code`/name; WebSocket close notices are fixed strings. `/whoami` display fields are capped; `avatarUrl` is null unless `https:`; `user.login` must be a GitHub login (`[A-Za-z0-9-]{1,39}`, optionally `[bot]`), or the reply is refused, and a stored bookmark login failing that check reads as null.
  - **Reply sizes are bounded:** `RemoteClient` reads every body through a byte cap and aborts the request past it — 64 KB for `/healthz`, `/whoami` and error bodies, 2 MB for RPC — and refuses a larger declared `content-length` before reading, so a hostile host can't exhaust main's memory during the unauthenticated probe or the 15 s poll.
  - **No host-driven clipboard writes:** OSC 52 from a remote tab is swallowed, never written (§8 *Copy and paste*).
  - **Remote automations run on their host:** desktop automations refuse remote targets, and remote sessions emit no desktop `session.idle` (§8 *Automations*).
  - **Plain `http://`** to a non-loopback host shows a one-line warning on the probe card and in the consent dialog, because the bearer crosses the wire.
  - **The desktop greys controls but enforces nothing:** watch-only, New-session gating, End session and tier badges are UX; the host enforces tiers per frame and CLOSE ownership.
  - **Federated reads** (remote-workspaces (d)) send a host only its own cursors and limits and session ids it returned; no local row, id or text. Federated search sends only the query's embedding, model id and limit, and only to the hosts in the Sessions pane's scope (the search box names them); an embedding approximately reveals the query, so the user decides by scope. Main keeps no cursor state, so the renderer can send any well-formed cursor to a bookmarked host under the user's own bearer (accepted: the host scopes every read to its workspace and tier). Replies are validated and capped (`federation/remoteRows.ts`: ids `[A-Za-z0-9_.:-]{1,128}`, safe non-negative integers, finite non-negative numbers (`usd`, `costSeries`; `tokenSeries` are safe non-negative integers), `oneLine` text capped at 500/128/64/2000, ≤ 200 rows, ≤ 20 tags of 64, ≤ 20 top tools, ≤ 50 calls, ≤ 200 series points); a bad row or recent tool call is dropped, a bad envelope marks the host unavailable (`protocol`). Remote text renders as React text only; the cloud glyph and titles use the bookmark's name, not the reply's.
- **fleet-host authenticates every request** except `/healthz` and `/leader`, and enforces tiers per route and per frame on the host (§7 *Remote host*); the desktop's greying is UX only.
- **fleet-host never stores, logs or echoes a bearer token.** The verify and negative caches are keyed by the token's SHA-256 and rebuilt with the provider on every applied reload. An open socket keeps its raw token in memory for its lifetime, to re-verify it every 60 s. Error bodies are generic; refusal detail goes to the redacted log.
- **Viewers' bearer tokens do enter a remote container,** in fleet-host's memory (verified per request, held by open sockets for re-verify); nothing else of a person's credentials does. `CREATE` args are rebuilt by the host, so a viewer can't append claude flags. The broker stays on its Unix socket: `fleet-host-entry` refuses to start with `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_TCP_PORT` set. Plain `http://` host URLs are allowed, with a desktop warning (remote-workspaces §13 Q6 is open).
- **Known risk: fleet-host, the broker and every claude session share uid 1000.** Anyone at `steer` or above can run commands in a session, and so can signal fleet-host, read its `/proc/<pid>` (environ, and memory where ptrace is allowed, which holds the raw tokens of open sockets), or kill it and listen on :8080 during the restart window to collect the Bearer tokens of reconnecting viewers. Interim mitigation: `fleet-host-entry` refuses to start fleet-host when anything already listens on `FLEET_HOST_PORT` (checked in `/proc/net/tcp{,6}` before every start, including restarts) and exits the container instead, so a squatter ends the pod rather than inheriting its traffic. This does not stop `/proc` reads or the kill itself. Until fleet-host runs under a separate uid, grant `steer` only to people trusted with every other viewer's GitHub token.
- **The shared uid also defeats the host's other controls.** A session (uid 1000, the pod's identity) can:
  - read the App key file (0400, uid 1000) and mint any token the installation allows (org-wide for `sumer-fleet-agent`), or ask the credential socket for any repo `github.repositories` permits;
  - mint an RDS IAM token through the pod's identity and write to the host DB directly, bypassing the lease fence. The fence protects against stale fleet-host processes, not against sessions;
  - read the host config, and fleet-host's environment (inherited, and through `/proc/<fleet-host>/environ`). `db:` refuses static passwords, but a password or connection string in an env var is exposed the same way; `aws-rds-iam` is the only production form, and its tokens are short-lived and minted from the pod's identity, which a session already holds.

  The App helper limits token lifetime and scope for well-behaved sessions and keeps the key out of env, logs and process arguments; it is not a boundary against a session. The fix is the host/workspace split (§11): fleet-host, the credential server and the Secret in a host pod, the sessions in a separate workspace pod reaching credentials over the mesh.
- **The host MCP socket** (§7 *Remote host*, *Host MCP*) is read-only except the inbox and automation tools, serves only the hosted workspace's rows, and takes its caller from fleet-host, never the wire. Its trust boundary is `cred.sock`'s: any session in the workspace container can connect, nothing else can. In the single-container layout it is 0600 in a 0700 directory, and the shared-uid caveats above apply (a session can do anything fleet-host can). In the container split it is 0660 in `prep`'s fleet-host-owned `mcp/` directory (group r-x only), so a session can connect but can't plant anything at its path. What a session can still do: create files in the shared `/run/fleet` directory (not in `mcp/`) and fill its tmpfs, and dial `broker.sock`, `cred.sock` and `mcp.sock`, all of which it can already reach.
- **The embedder sidecar parses session-written transcript text** as the holder's uid (§7 *Remote host*, *Embedder*). Its input to the model is token ids into a fixed ONNX graph; the residual risk (a tokenizer or onnxruntime bug reached through crafted text) is the same class as ingest's JSONL parser. In single-container mode a session can also kill the sidecar, which turns search off until the next holder.
- **A printed token is visible.** A token a session prints (`gh auth token` inside a `gh` child, `git credential fill`) reaches every viewer of that session for the token's lifetime (≤ 1 h). Redaction (§7 *Remote host*, *Logging*) limits only the copies the host stores (ingest) and re-sends (`/events`). The desktop's own transcript ingest (`jsonlWatcher`) stores lines unredacted.
- **Don't configure a storing git credential helper for github.com in a hosted workspace.** `/etc/gitconfig` resets the helper list, but a user- or repo-level `credential.helper store` (or `cache`) set after it still runs, and git hands it the token on `store`, persisting it in `~/.git-credentials` beyond its use.
- **Renderer is isolated.** `contextIsolation: true`, `nodeIntegration: false`. No `require`, no `process`, no `fs` from renderer code.
- **`sandbox: false`** because preload uses `ipcRenderer`. The renderer itself still has no Node access.
- **Renderer cannot escape the IPC surface.** It can: list/create/start/stop/remove workspaces carrying the fleet label, list/get/set/delete per-workspace secrets, attach/detach a PTY. It cannot: shell out, read arbitrary files, touch other Docker containers, hit the network with Node APIs.
- **Workspace isolation is Docker's.** No additional sandboxing layered on top. Containers run as the host user's UID (via `User: '<uid>:<gid>'`) and can write to the bind-mounted host workspace as that user.
- **Host-private zone — default-deny container exposure.** `<userData>` is the main process's private domain: the SQLite state DB, `config.json`, `error.log`, the safeStorage vault, and every workspace's durable transcript mirror (`<userData>/state/<id>/_history/`). **Nothing under `<userData>` is bind-mounted into a container** except a workspace's *own* `.claude` dir, its `.claude.json`, its broker socket dir, its **own per-id read-only MCP socket dir** `<userData>/mcp/<id>/` (§11/#117 — the per-id leaf only; the shared parent `<userData>/mcp/` is never mounted, so a container cannot reach a sibling's socket), and — in OAuth mode — the shared credentials/remote-settings files. The docker socket is held only by the main process — it is never mounted into a workspace container, so there is no docker-in-docker escape path. The invariant: cross-workspace or sensitive data is **mediated by the main process** (the MCP server is exactly that mediator — it exposes the DB read-only, never the file), never exposed by a raw bind mount. So a workspace can read fleet-wide *session/event* data **through the MCP server's read-only tools**, but can never read another workspace's transcripts, secrets, or DB file off the filesystem. This is why the durable transcript mirror (§11) lives under `<userData>` and not in the container-visible fleet root. The **loadout library** (`<userData>/loadouts/`, §7) follows the same rule: never bind-mounted, so a workspace sees only the *installed snapshot* in its own `.claude/`; loadout discovery for a workspace's Claude is **mediated** by the read-only MCP `list_loadouts`/`get_loadout` tools, never by exposing the library folder.
- **Extra mounts are user-chosen host folders, bounded.** A container workspace may bind extra host folders, but `validateExtraMounts` refuses (after realpath) anything inside or containing `<userData>`; under the fleet root it denies by default, allowing only `shared` and the workspace's own `<id>` folder, and refuses an ancestor of the fleet root — so the host-private zone and sibling private folders stay unreachable. It also refuses any folder equal to or containing a Docker socket's folder (literal and resolved through a symlinked socket), and any folder equal to, inside, or containing `~/.docker` (`dockerSocketDirs()`), keeping the invariant that the Docker socket is never mounted into a workspace container, and refuses network UNC host paths (`\\server\…`). WSL paths (`\\wsl$\<distro>\…`, win32 only; never a `docker-desktop*` distro) are resolved inside the distro in one fixed-script `wsl.exe` call that also returns the mount table, and refused when a path segment has characters Windows would re-parse in the `\\wsl$` Source (`\`, `:`, control characters, trailing `.`/space, …), at or under `/mnt` or at, under, or containing any `9p`/`drvfs`/`v9fs`/`virtiofs` mount (Windows drives, including `<userData>`, and Docker Desktop's `/mnt/wsl` socket integration) or `cifs`/`smb3`/`smbfs`/`nfs*` network mount, at or under `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`, `/run`, `/var/run`, at a whole home folder, at any ancestor of those, or with a `.docker` segment; a `<userData>` or fleet root inside the same distro gets the same userData and fleet-root rules on its in-distro path. `toDockerMounts` repeats the same check at container-create time against the resolved realpath, closing the window where any symlink in the host path gets re-pointed between save and create; the residual gap is that Docker itself follows symlinks when it performs the bind (there is no fd-based mount API), so a retarget landing between that check and Docker's own mount is not caught.
- **The one deliberate cross-container surface is `<fleetRoot>/shared` → `/shared` (rw in every container).** It exists so workspaces can exchange files on purpose; treat it accordingly — **secrets must not be written to `/shared`**, since every workspace can read it.
- **Git-synced folders live under `/shared`**, so the no-secrets rule applies to their repos too. A synced repo is readable by every workspace on the node and by everyone with access to the remote. Sync credentials stay in the syncing workspace's env and are never written into `/shared`. Because any workspace can edit a clone's `.git`, the sync script treats clones as untrusted: it never runs their hooks or fsmonitor and refuses clones whose `.git/config` strays from an allowlist or from the configured remote (§8 *Sharing folders between nodes*). Otherwise a planted `core.fsmonitor`, hook or `pushurl` would run code in, or leak the token of, the syncing workspace.
- **OAuth credentials are shared across workspaces by design.** In `oauth` mode all workspaces file-bind one `.credentials.json` (one login covers the fleet), so a token present in one container is the same token in every OAuth workspace. `apikey` mode is per-workspace (the key is injected as an env var, visible only inside that container). Either way, a container legitimately holds its *own* auth — the boundary being protected is *other* workspaces' data and the host-private zone, not a workspace's view of its own credentials.
- **`plan_usage` crosses workspace boundaries but is structurally aggregate-only.** `foldPlanUsage` has no per-workspace field — only fleet-total USD/tokens, model/backend breakdowns, and the latest anchor. Totals cross the boundary; content and per-workspace detail never do.
- **External link handling**: `setWindowOpenHandler` denies in-app navigation and opens external URLs via `shell.openExternal` — only `http:`/`https:` (`externalUrl.ts:isOpenableExternalUrl`); `about:`, `file:`, `javascript:` and custom protocols are dropped.
- **Markdown text fields** (`components/Markdown.tsx`, `react-markdown` + `remark-gfm` + `remark-breaks`): todo bodies, loadout instructions and proposal rationales render as block markdown (GFM tables, lists, code, autolinked URLs; single newlines stay line breaks so plain-text bodies look unchanged); loadout, automation, workspace and run-param descriptions and proposal notes render inline (emphasis, code, links only; other structure is flattened to text). Because most of this text is untrusted (registries, workspaces, PR-derived automation output): raw HTML is skipped, only `http(s)` URLs survive `urlTransform` (links open via the window-open handler above), and images are never loaded (no CSP, so a remote image would be a beacon), with their alt text shown instead. Toasts, error/probe messages, OS notifications and script output stay plain text.
- **Port-forward preview — loopback-only, no new published ports.** The host `PortForward` listener binds `127.0.0.1` only — not `0.0.0.0`, no LAN exposure. The broker dials `127.0.0.1:<port>` inside its own container only — never an arbitrary host or IP; `port` is a constrained uint16. No new Docker published ports are created on any platform: Linux/macOS ride the existing bind-mounted Unix socket; Windows rides the existing loopback-TCP broker port. The new `DIAL`/`LISTPORTS` frames do not widen who can reach the broker (still only the host-owned Unix socket or loopback-TCP on Windows), preserving the broker's existing no-auth posture.
- **Port kill is host-mediated with no cross-workspace path.** `ports:kill(workspaceId, port)` routes a `KILLPORT` frame over the *target workspace's own broker socket* only — the host never routes kill signals across workspace boundaries. The victim PID is resolved by the broker at kill time from the live socket (never accepted from the host), eliminating any PID-reuse hazard from the host supplying a stale PID. The broker then kills the resolved owner's **command subtree** (bounded by the owning session's PTY root, or the owner itself when unattributed), so the blast radius is confined to that session's process tree — it can never climb to the session shell or another session.
- **Vault availability + the WSL plaintext trade**: the main process probes secret-storage usability once (`vault:available`). On macOS/Windows and Linux-with-a-keyring this is real OS-backed encryption. On Linux with **no** keyring (e.g. WSL), `vault.ts` opts into safeStorage's plaintext backend (`setUsePlainTextEncryption(true)`) so `vault:available` returns true and API-key auth works — but secret values are then stored **base64, not encrypted**, in `<userData>/secrets.enc`. This is a deliberate trade: the prior `keytar` path failed entirely on WSL, and the file already lives in the per-user `userData` dir. If storage is somehow unusable even so, `vault:available` returns false → the env editor disables the per-row "secret" toggle (a row can still be a plain env var, value in the manifest) and the auth-mode picker degrades to OAuth-only unless `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is supplied as plain env, with a `BottomBar` notice. (Surfacing the *plaintext* case distinctly in the UI is a future nicety, not yet wired.)
- **Proposals (#407 Phase 5a):** a workspace can propose but never approve; only low-risk edits from a container workspace you marked trusted apply on their own, and each stays revertible.
- **Script steps (#407 Phase 7)** deliberately relax "workspaces never reach the host" for **user-approved host steps only**: a `runIn: host` step runs a shell on this computer with a clean env and only its declared secrets. No proposal containing a host step is ever low-risk (trusted workspaces can't auto-apply it, and any edit to it needs approval again); approval cards flag it in red ("runs a script on your computer (<shell>)"). Container steps run in the automations workspace (or a named container workspace); secrets are injected into that one exec, never set on the container or written to disk by the host. Script text never contains args; they arrive as env.
- **Automations (#407 Phase 3)** run only from definitions the user created or approved; their workspace effects (`hostPostToWorkspace`, `hostEnsureRunning` in `ipc.ts`) are not reachable over MCP. Workspaces can `list_automations` (what they may run or are prompted by) and `run_automation` only when listed in `manual.allowFrom`; their args reach prompts as quoted data.
- **Notifications are host-mediated too (#407 Phase 2).** `notify` shares `post_todo`'s permission and hourly cap; a `todo_id` must be one the caller may see. Only the user's rules decide delivery, and a workspace can neither read nor change rules, pause or downtime.
- **Inbox writes are host-mediated (#407).** The fleet-state MCP gains three write-path tools, `post_todo`, `update_todo` and `list_todos`, whose effects run in the main process (`inboxService.ts`). The caller is the accepting listener's workspace id (ambient identity, never a wire argument), so `source` can't be forged across containers; a local workspace's identity carries the same spoofability caveat as committee identity. Gates: `inbox.canPost` (default true) and a per-workspace sliding one-hour cap (`maxPostsPerHour`, default 60), where an over-cap post is **rejected** with `retryAfterMs` and an `inbox-post-rejected` error-log row. `update_todo` only touches todos the caller sourced (or did the work for, via `via_workspace`). `list_todos` returns only what the caller sourced, is linked to (`workspace_id`), or did the work for, unless `inbox.canReadAll`. Nothing about the inbox is bind-mounted; `inbox.db` is host-private like `state.db`.
- **Knowledge claims are an intentional, agent-authored exception to workspace isolation (#483).** A `fleet`-visibility claim (the default) is deliberately readable by every workspace via `search_claims` — it's a short statement an agent *chose to publish*, not transcript content, and is not subject to the §9 cross-workspace isolation rule the typed read tools and `query` enforce. A `private` claim stays visible only to its authoring workspace. The secret filter in `record_claim` (`claimValidate.ts`) is a **backstop, not a guarantee** — the standing rule and the capture prompt both forbid secrets in the first place; agents must not rely on the filter to catch what they shouldn't have written. Capture forks (headless `claude -p --resume --fork-session`) can use exactly `mcp__claude-fleet-state__record_claim` and `__search_claims` and nothing else. `--allowedTools` alone would only pre-approve those two (user/project allow rules such as `Bash(*)` or `mcp__claude-fleet-state`, and a `bypassPermissions`/`auto` default mode, would still apply), so `captureCommand.ts:captureArgs` also passes `--tools ""` (no built-in tools: no Bash, Read, Edit, Agent…), `--permission-mode dontAsk` (anything not pre-approved is denied, never prompted; overrides the user's default mode), `--disallowedTools` naming every other fleet-state tool (derived from `mcpServer.ts` `TOOLS` at startup; deny beats any settings allow rule), and `--strict-mcp-config` with a fleet-state-only `--mcp-config` (no claude.ai connectors or other user/project MCP servers). Setting sources are deliberately not narrowed (user settings can carry auth/endpoint env), so user hooks still run in a fork, as they do in the session itself. Verified empirically on 2.1.281 (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-04-knowledge-claims-gate-results.md`, *C1 tool-restriction verification*). A fork only ever sees the conversation its own session already holds. Provenance (`workspace_id`, `via`, and — via a validated `capture_run` token — `session_id`) is **host-stamped**: `workspace_id` comes only from the MCP caller identity (`ctx.callerId`), never a wire-supplied value, so an agent can never forge another workspace's or session's authorship of a claim.
- **Capture settings are self-service but cost-gated (#483 §5a).** A workspace can read and change only its own capture settings (`ctx.callerId`); it can never change another workspace's, the global switch, or the daily cap (UI only). A change that raises cost, publishes privately-captured claims (`captureVisibility` `private` → `fleet`), or deletes transcripts sooner (a lower `transcriptRetentionDays`) needs the user's approval unless the workspace is trusted (`classifyNeedsApproval`), and every change is logged and visible.
- **Workspace skills are mediated metadata.** `list_workspace_skills` / `get_workspace_skill` read skill files the host already owns (private folders, per-workspace `.claude`, local roots) and return them read-only; no new mounts. Exposure is opt-out per workspace (`inbox.exposeSkills`), and bodies are labelled as untrusted data. Every root dir and file is **realpath-contained** to its workspace (project root, or the user `.claude` dir), the same rule on every backend — a skill symlinked in from outside the workspace root (e.g. a dotfiles repo linked into `~/.claude/skills` on a native local workspace) is skipped with a warning, not read. Containment is checked *before* the file is ever opened (realpath the name, require it's inside the base), so the host never opens an escaping target in the first place; a second realpath + `dev`/`ino` match against the already-open fd then catches a genuine race (the name getting swapped out from under the check). That post-open check makes file **contents** race-proof — the bytes returned always come from the inode that passed containment. A *directory listing* isn't equally race-proof: a container can swap a directory for a symlink between the per-root containment check and the `readdir` that lists it, so an escaping entry's name can briefly surface in that listing before its file read is refused. The fix is that a racing swap can at most cause an entry to be **skipped**, never to be read, and skip warnings carry no names: per scan, per-file escapes are counted and rolled into a single aggregate warning (`N entries skipped (resolve outside the workspace)`, or the singular `1 entry skipped (resolves outside the workspace)`) rather than naming each file. A whole-root escape (the root dir itself resolves outside) still gets its own warning, but that one names only the root's kind/source (e.g. "project commands skipped"), never a file. Warnings/errors over MCP carry only that and errno codes, never host paths or file names.
- **Local-workspace launcher trust (#253).** The `custom` launcher mode executes a **user-supplied command** on the host, with the user's own privileges — this is the same trust the local backend already grants: `claude` itself has host-equivalent reach, and the user chose both the binary and the working directory. No new privilege boundary is crossed; the command template is stored in the user's own manifest under `<userData>`. WSL-mode workspaces inherit the existing local-workspace security caveat: MCP identity is not unspoofable (the host-side socket accepts connections from the same user, and a compromised distro process could dial it), so `assertControl` keeps refusing local workspaces for committee control — they never appear as valid committee targets.

```
claude-fleet/

├── README.md                          # one-paragraph project summary + dev commands
├── docs/
│   └── SPEC.md                        # this file
├── .claude/
│   └── rules/
│       └── spec-maintenance.md        # the rule
├── docker/
│   ├── Dockerfile                     # BASE runner (multi-stage, builds broker); lean + standalone
│   ├── versions.yaml                  # pinned tool versions (source of truth for the scripts)
│   ├── scripts/                       # shared, standalone, arch-aware installers (one per toolset)
│   │   ├── _arch.sh                   #   uname → amd64/arm64 + x86_64/aarch64
```

## 11. Open decisions

These are decided in spirit but not yet implemented. When you implement one, move it out of this section and into the relevant body section above.

### Remote host: what remains
The holder runs its workspace's automations and serves the fleet-state MCP (§7 *Remote host*, *Host automations*, *Host MCP*). Still open:
- **Approximate (HNSW) search:** still exact. Revisit when host p50 exceeds 250 ms or one scope exceeds 250k rows, which becomes likely once (e) publishes partitions to a host.
- **Host checklist for (d2):** after the image rolls out, run `fleet-host embed-check` in the pod; check that `search_transcripts` from a session returns hits; check that a host with `search: { embedder: false }` shows "search off" in the desktop's transcript search; raise the host pod's memory request by 256 Mi.
- **Host checklist for (d1):** re-run `fleet-host bootstrap` so the `query` roles and the `<app>_query` login exist; the infra grant `rds-db:connect` on `fleet_agent_query` (platform-ops#46); verify `query` from a session sees only the hosted workspace. Non-IAM hosts get no `query`.
- **Approvals and notifications** ((g)): `automations:*`, `proposals:*` and `inbox:*` RPC channels on `startHolder`'s `automations` seam, approving host proposals (sessions can't propose today; definitions come only from the host config), and `notify` and run-failure notifications through desktop policy.
- **Script steps on the host:** refused until the broker can run a script in the workspace container (a broker exec op); after the host/workspace split, only fleet-host's pod holds the DB role.
- **The two-pod MCP listener:** decided in the host/workspace split (below): fleet-host serves the MCP on TCP :8082 with the caller fixed to the hosted workspace, the mesh admits only the workspace pod's SA, and sessions' bridge dials it with `CLAUDE_FLEET_MCP_TCP` and no token line. Nothing in the tools changes.
- **`--mcp-config` precedence:** whether fleet-host's inline `claude-fleet-state` wins over a server of the same name in `~/.claude.json` on the shared volume is unverified (`claude mcp list`/`get` ignore `--mcp-config`); it is on the host checklist.

Deployments keep one replica without overlap (`maxSurge: 0`, `maxUnavailable: 1`); with `db:` the fenced lease makes an overlap safe, but the invariant stays until it is deliberately relaxed. A host without `db:` must run one replica. `CLOSE` owners are in memory and lost on restart, so afterwards no session reports `mine` and the desktop offers End session for none. KILLPORT can still end a session whose root process itself listens on the port, because the broker's `LIST`/`LISTPORTS` don't expose session root pids (#489).

### Host/workspace split
Decided, not built: fleet-host runs in a host pod (SA `fleet-agent`, Pod Identity role `fleet-host` with `rds-db:connect` as `fleet_agent` and `fleet_agent_query`; uid 1001; the App key, the ConfigMap, :8080) and the broker and sessions in a workspace pod (SA `fleet-agent-workspace`, role `fleet-workspace` chained to `infra-llmbot-viewonly`; uid 1000; no key, no DB access, no ConfigMap), from one image. Nothing is shared but the transcripts (EFS, read-only on the host). The broker listens on TCP :7070 and the host pod serves credentials on :8081 and the fleet-state MCP on :8082, all behind Istio mTLS (`STRICT`) and AuthorizationPolicies: only `fleet-agent` reaches :7070, only `fleet-agent-workspace` reaches :8081/:8082. The broker has no authentication of its own, so its security rests on the mesh. `fleet-host-entry` takes a required mode (`host`, `workspace`, `single`). This closes the shared-uid risks in §9. Design, ports and pod specs: [`superpowers/specs/2026-11-05-host-container-split-design.md`](superpowers/specs/2026-11-05-host-container-split-design.md); the plan `superpowers/plans/2026-11-05-host-container-split.md` predates the two-pod layout and is to be rewritten.

### Inbox, automations & desktop notifications (#407)
All eight phases are in the body (§6 *Inbox* / *Automations*, §7 *Inbox store*, §8 *Inbox*, §9). The design is in [`superpowers/specs/2026-10-20-inbox-automations-design.md`](superpowers/specs/2026-10-20-inbox-automations-design.md); overnight build decisions are in `superpowers/plans/2026-10-20-overnight-decisions.md`. Still open from that design:
- **Test run** for drafts (runs once with todos/notifications going to a scratch view, never the inbox). It needs a scratch sink in `inboxService` attribution.
- **Export:** "Save as loadout…" / "Add to loadout" from an automation's Edit menu.
- **Loadout updates:** a per-automation Keep mine / Take update / Diff choice (today: unedited ones update, edited ones are kept), and "Ask Claude about this" on loadout install/update reviews.
- **Inbox chip ⋮ menu** (New session · Edit workspace · Restart · Recreate) for the automations workspace; Edit / Stop using live on the Automations tab banner today.
- **Composer extras:** a per-draft drafter picker and requestable secrets.

### Per-container Claude Code state visibility on the host
Each container gets its own host-side state dir, bind-mounted into the container at `/home/fleet/.claude/`. This is the foundation for the observability watcher, sessions table, durable mirror, and permission-request log — all of which read events from `<state-dir>/projects/-workspace/*.jsonl` directly off the host filesystem.

**Decisions made:**
- **Host layout**: `<userData>/state/<container-name>/.claude/`, where `<userData>` is Electron's `app.getPath('userData')` (`~/.config/claude-fleet/` on Linux). State dirs are keyed by container name — recreating a container with the same name reuses its prior state. Use a different name to start fresh.
- **Container layout**: the host state dir is bind-mounted at `/home/fleet/.claude/` read-write. The container's `WorkingDir` is `/workspace`, so Claude Code writes JSONLs to `~/.claude/projects/-workspace/<session-uuid>.jsonl` — the sanitized-cwd is deterministically `-workspace`.
- **UID/GID**: the container runs as the host user's UID/GID via `User: '<uid>:<gid>'` set at create time. `HOME=/home/fleet` is set in the env so tooling that consults `HOME` finds the bind-mounted `.claude/` even when the runtime UID has no `/etc/passwd` entry. This replaces the earlier idea of baking UIDs at image-build time — the published runner image now has a single fixed UID for the in-image `fleet` user, and the host's UID is supplied at container start.
- **Create-time behavior**: if `<userData>/state/<id>/.claude/` does not exist, the main process creates it (owned by the host user) before starting the container. If it exists, it's reused as-is.
- **Removal behavior**: when the user removes a container, a confirmation modal asks "Also delete this container's state dir?" with **Keep** as the default. Picking Delete recursively removes `<userData>/state/<id>/`. Picking Keep leaves the state intact so a future container with that name inherits it.

**Implementation:**
- `src/main/docker.ts createContainer`: add the state-dir bind to `HostConfig.Binds` alongside the existing workspace bind. Ensure the host dir exists (`mkdir -p`) with host-user ownership before starting the container.
- `src/main/docker.ts removeContainer`: take `opts: { deleteState: boolean }`. When true, recursively remove the state dir after the Docker container is removed.
- `src/main/ipc.ts`: extend `docker:remove` to accept the `deleteState` flag from the renderer.
- Removal-confirmation modal: implemented as `CloseContainerModal`, opened from the main-pane header's **Close…** button. See §8 "Close a container".
- Runner image (#5): the image is published to GHCR by CI (`ghcr.io/imioimi/claude-fleet/runner:latest`). The app pulls it on first use; UID is handled at container start via `User`, not baked at image-build time.

**Open:**
- **State-dir name sanitization.** Container names can contain characters that are invalid or hazardous as path components (`/`, `:`, leading dots). Validate/restrict at the create form (`[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+`) so the name maps cleanly to a directory name. Lands with the create-container UX (#4).
- **Cross-user / multi-host portability.** State is tied to the host user (UID typically 1000). Moving a state dir to another machine requires matching UIDs or a chown. Out of scope; document if it becomes a real workflow.
- **Settings reset.** `.claude/` will eventually also hold `settings.json`, custom commands, file checkpoints, tasks state. All of it survives container recreate by default. If we later want a "reset settings without losing project history" affordance, decide what's keepable vs. wipeable.

### Observability layer: cost, tokens, tool calls
Per-session cost and token counts derived from Claude transcript JSONL events. **Status: shipped (#2).** Foundation + cost rollup + pane + slot consumers + live push + per-model context window + per-tab mapping + tool-call detail (duration/status) + per-turn cost series, plus the expanded rail (sparkline, scope toggle, Fleet view) and per-terminal context rows. Residual (out of this issue's scope): richer per-tab state and ingesting subagent JSONLs.

**Shipped:**
- The JSONL→SQLite cache + watcher (§7 *JSONL→SQLite cache*), the `observability:eventsForSession` catch-up IPC (§6), and the `chokidar`+`better-sqlite3` runtime pieces (§4). Watcher tails every workspace's transcripts and ingests new lines idempotently into `events`, updating `sessions` with derived metadata.
- `summaryForWorkspace` (§6) plus the right-rail pane (§5) — title, model, event count, last-activity, prominent USD, token totals, top tools.
- Cost rollup (`src/main/pricing.ts` + `costForSession` / `costForWorkspace` in `src/main/db.ts`, §6 IPCs). USD is derived per `(model, service_tier)` group with hardcoded Claude 4.x rates — Opus $15/$75/$1.50/$18.75, Sonnet $3/$15/$0.30/$3.75, Haiku $1/$5/$0.10/$1.25 per 1M tokens (input / output / cache-read / cache-creation). Standard tier full price, batch tier 50%, unknown model or tier degrades to $0 + one-time `console.warn`. Unit-tested in `src/main/pricing.test.ts` via Vitest.
- Slot consumers (#34): chip secondary line ("active 2m ago" / "idle 1h ago" from `summary.lastActiveAt`) and terminal-pane context bar (fill driven by `summary.lastTurnContextTokens / summary.contextWindowTokens`). Both read from App.tsx's shared summary map so chip + pane + bar share one source of truth (see §5 *Centralized observability distribution*).
- Live summary push: `JsonlWatcher` extends `EventEmitter`, emits `'ingest'` on every batch that inserts ≥1 new event, and `ipc.ts` broadcasts `observability:summary` to every BrowserWindow. The renderer's `onSummary` subscription updates the shared map in <100ms of the JSONL flush. A 30s safety poll covers missed events and refreshes relative-time displays.
- Per-model context window (`src/main/contextWindow.ts`): replaces the renderer's hardcoded 200K. Defaults to 200K per Claude 4.x family, recognizes the `[1m]` marker in the model id, and auto-upgrades to 1M when observed usage already crossed 200K (handles the 1M beta header case, where the model string itself doesn't change). Plumbed through `WorkspaceSummary.contextWindowTokens`; the context bar's 80% compaction tick is now positioned correctly because the limit is data-driven. Unit-tested in `src/main/contextWindow.test.ts`.
- Per-tab mapping (`broker_sessions` table): each terminal tab's broker session id maps to a specific claude session UUID. **Learned deterministically at spawn time (#195):** whenever a backend actually spawns claude for a tab (broker CREATE for docker, fresh pty spawn for local), the host decides the claude UUID up front — `resumeOf` when resuming, else a generated UUID passed as `--session-id` — and writes the mapping before the process starts. A plain re-attach to a live claude never relearns. Rows carry a **`verified`** flag (v7): deterministic learns set 1; every pre-#198 row migrated as 0. Unverified rows still serve observability attribution but are refused as resume targets (`lookupVerifiedBrokerSession` / `sessions:resolveResumeTarget`). **Phantom-safe remaps (#170):** a signal-driven remap (SessionStart hook, or a re-CREATE of a tab that still holds a real conversation) goes through `recordBrokerSessionMapping`, which only overwrites the committed `claude_session_id` when it's safe to — a fresh tab, a target that already has a transcript, or a committed id that is itself a rowless phantom. When the tab holds a real conversation and the new id has written nothing yet (the classic `/clear`-then-abandon case), the remap is parked in a **`pending_claude_session_id`** column (v10) rather than committed; `ingestBatch` promotes it (verified=1) the instant that session produces its first transcript line. This stops an abandoned cleared session — a phantom with no `sessions` row — from black-holing the tab (null title, dropped from the *Open* list, blank observability). An in-memory set of pending claude ids (rehydrated from the column on open) keeps the promotion check off the hot ingest path. The legacy passive path (`pendingAttaches.ts`: record a pending attach, pair FIFO-oldest when `JsonlWatcher` emits `'new-session'`) remains only as a fallback for sessions the host did not name; production attach paths no longer feed it. `'new-session'` fires on the first sighting of a primary transcript in each watcher lifetime, so at launch for every existing transcript; `pairNewSession` logs a miss only when the queue holds attaches for other workspaces. It has **no TTL** (claude writes its first JSONL only when the user types, which can be hours later) and FIFO pairing across >1 pending entries is a guess — logged as `mapping-ambiguous-consume`, and a mapping overwritten with a different claude id as `mapping-remapped` (both `warn`, in `errors`). The guess-based pairing is what cross-wired a tab to the wrong conversation in #195, which is why the deterministic path replaced it. Mapping persists in SQLite so re-attaching after an app restart (when broker still has claude alive and no new JSONL appears) still resolves to the right session. **Fallback semantics:** `summaryForBrokerSession` returns null when no mapping exists, and the renderer surfaces the ObservabilityPane's empty state directly — no workspace-summary fallback. We tried a conditional fallback (loaded tabs fall back, fresh tabs don't); it produced two user-visible bugs (new session inheriting previous tab's data; switching between unmapped tabs showing the same content on both — "doesn't update"). Per-tab semantics now never inherit from sibling tabs. **Known consequence:** tabs created before this table existed have no mapping and stay empty until recreated; the workspace's overall activity is still surfaced on the chip subline and the right-rail pane's USD/eventCount via the tab's own session once a mapping lands. Unit-tested in `src/main/pendingAttaches.test.ts`; e2e regressions in `tests/smoke.spec.ts` (`broker_sessions: a single pending attach`, `broker_sessions: mapping is learned even when the user types many minutes after attaching the tab`, `broker_sessions: multi-tab attaches that interleave`, `ObservabilityPane: clicking +`, `ObservabilityPane: switching between two loaded-from-inventory tabs`, `summaryForBrokerSession: returns null for an unmapped broker session`). **Diagnostic signals:** two structured events are recorded into the `errors` table (reachable via `list_errors`) to make a blank rail diagnosable without grepping logs: a `warn`-level signal fires once per unique `(workspace, tab, outcome)` when `summaryForBrokerSession` cannot resolve — as type `mapping-unresolved` when no broker→claude mapping exists, or `mapping-stale-session` when a mapping exists but the claude session has no DB rows; `new-session-dropped` (level `info`) fires when a `'new-session'` JSONL event arrives with no pending attach to pair with (the common case now that the host names sessions at CREATE time; `'new-session'` itself is emitted **at most once per session id** via the watcher's `announcedSessions` ledger, so a transcript re-sighted after its `files` entry was dropped — chokidar unlink→add, unregister, stat failure — can't re-fire it into a flood, #243); `mapping-deferred` (level `info`) fires when a remap is parked pending a transcript instead of committed (#170). Neither changes the "no workspace fallback" rail behavior; they are purely diagnostic.

**Outstanding:**
- **Richer tab states.** Tab dots are live / ended today. `idle` and `needs-input` states want the permission-request log (#11) and a recency signal before they can be wired meaningfully.
- **Pricing refresh process.** `pricing.ts` is hand-maintained. When Anthropic publishes new rates, the constants need updating. Consider an annual recheck cadence and/or a comment-pinned source URL.
- **Plan-usage ledger deferred pieces.** Cross-device/account sync is a permanent non-goal — `usage_anchors` are machine-local. The observability-rail UI widget that consumes `observability:planUsage` is a follow-up (the IPC channel ships now; the renderer consumer does not). `estimate.capUsd` calibration from multiple anchors (curve-fitting) is also deferred — today `basis:'seed'` uses a single anchor with a fixed plan assumption.

### Sessions table

**Status: shipped.** Global, workspace-filterable list of every Claude session the watcher has indexed, each resumable via `claude --resume <id>`, renamable, and deletable. Implementation lives in the body: §6 *Sessions* (`sessions:list`/`rename`/`delete`/`resume` IPC), §6 *PTY* (`resumeOf` attach), §7 *Session inventory* (`resumeOf` field) + *JSONL→SQLite cache* (the `sessions` table is the index; JSONL stays the source of truth), and §8 *Browse & resume a past session* (the `SessionsPane` UI). It's workspace-keyed (not container-keyed) — sessions outlive any one container, and eligibility is "workspace manifest still exists."

**Resume mechanism.** The host knows the Claude session UUID up front, so the broker→claude mapping is written **directly** at CREATE time rather than via the watcher's pending-attach queue — `claude --resume` appends to the existing `<uuid>.jsonl`, so no `new-session` event ever fires. The broker gained an optional `args` field on `CREATE` (`broker/internal/proto`) threaded through `Manager.Create` → `newSession` → `exec.Command(claude, args...)`; the host passes `["--resume", "<uuid>"]` (or, for a fresh session, `["--session-id", "<uuid>"]` — same mechanism, #195).

**Broker env-var contract.** Every claude PTY spawned by the broker receives two env vars injected by `newSession` on top of the inherited container env: `TERM=xterm-256color` (required for the TUI) and `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_SESSION_ID=<id>` (the broker session id — the same stable id the host uses as the tab key in `sessions.json`). The local backend (`localSessions.ts`) sets the same `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_SESSION_ID` env var on every local PTY spawn so that local workspaces participate in the same identity chain. In-container hooks (`session-report.sh`, `summarize.sh`) read `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_SESSION_ID` to pair their MCP calls with the tab that owns the PTY, without any out-of-band IPC. This is the env-var side of the broker→claude identity chain (#207).

**SessionStart hook → verified mapping (`session-report.sh`).** Registered under `SessionStart` in `hooks.settings.json` (fires on startup, resume, **and `/clear`** — the drift case). The hook reads `session_id` from the hook payload and `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_SESSION_ID` from env, then calls the `report_session_mapping` MCP tool over the workspace's own socket. The host records the mapping via the **phantom-safe** path (`recordBrokerSessionMapping`): it commits immediately (`verified=1`) when that's safe — a fresh tab, a reported id that already has a transcript, or a currently-committed id that is itself a rowless phantom (a broken mapping must never win) — and logs `mapping-remapped` when a live mapping is overwritten with a different id. Otherwise (the tab still holds a real conversation and the reported id has written nothing yet) the remap is **parked** in `pending_claude_session_id` instead of overwriting the live row, logged as `mapping-deferred` (info). This corrects `/clear` drift — after a clear, claude starts a new session id in the same tab and `SessionStart` reports it — without the #170 failure it originally introduced: a cleared session abandoned with zero turns is a *phantom* (`get_session` → null), and committing it black-holed the tab (every per-tab lookup resolved a rowless session → no title, dropped from the *Open* list, blank observability). The parked remap is promoted to the committed `claude_session_id` by `ingestBatch` the moment that session writes its first transcript line, so the tab follows the cleared conversation only once it is real. Container backend only; local backend currently lacks this hook.

**Open (residual):**
- **Auto-title (`ai_title`) — shipped, not a standalone LLM build.** This was once an open question ("which model / when / how much transcript"); it's moot. Two sources feed `ai_title` (§7 *Title derivation*): Claude Code's native one-shot `ai-title` line, and — for freshness on long sessions — a **rolling** `ai-title` line piggybacked on the `summarize.sh` chapter call (#170), so no dedicated titler is needed and there is no extra LLM cost. Residual **fragility** is only on the native line: it depends on an undocumented transcript line type, so a `claude` pin bump should re-verify it still appears (`tests/ai-title.spec.ts` is the guard). If Claude Code stops emitting it, the summarizer's rolling title still keeps sessions titled.
- **In-progress sessions.** The list shows active sessions too (they refresh on every ingest push). No distinct "live row" affordance vs. ended sessions yet — tab-state richness is tracked under *Observability layer*.
- **Deleting a live session's transcript.** `sessions:delete` unlinks the JSONL even if claude is still appending to it (rare — you'd be deleting the session you're in). On Linux the open fd keeps the inode alive, so claude keeps writing to the now-unlinked file until it reopens; the row reappears on the next new transcript. Acceptable for v1; revisit if it bites.

### Resumable sessions on workspace pause/resume — "expert workspaces"

The goal: **expert workspaces** that load a domain context once (an organization's architecture, a particular application's source, a body of documentation) and then sleep with that context intact. The user pauses the workspace, closes the app, comes back hours or days later, unpauses, and finds every session right where it was — same conversation history, same in-memory analyses, same MCP server state — ready to do a quick task and go back to sleep. Re-priming the context on every wake defeats the purpose.

**Status: shipped in two phases.** Phase 1 (sessions persistence + paused UI) and Phase 2 (in-container Go broker + host-side BrokerClient) are both landed. The relevant body sections describe the implementation; what remains here is just the residual open questions.

- Phase 1 see: §6 (Sessions IPC), §7 (Session inventory), §8 (Attach a terminal → Paused state).
- Phase 2 see: §5 (Architecture diagram + broker description), §6 (PTY IPC with `brokerSessionId`), §7 (broker socket bind), §10 (`broker/` Go module).

**Open (residual):**

- **Container restart policy — wired (`unless-stopped`, see §7 *Docker container shape*).** On a host reboot / daemon restart the container comes back and its broker re-launches. The broker's *in-memory* PTY state (live `claude` processes) is still lost — but the Sessions table's resume (§6 *PTY* `resumeOf`) rebuilds a session from its on-disk transcript, so "wake-and-go across reboots" now works without broker checkpoint/restore: the container is back automatically, and the user re-resumes from disk. True in-memory continuity across reboots (no re-priming) would still need session checkpoint/restore inside the broker — out of scope.
- **Clean-exit flush of sessions.json — not needed.** The renderer already persists `sessions.json` on every tab mutation (add/close/switch), and `ipcRenderer.invoke` dispatches the write to main synchronously, so there's no main-side unflushed inventory state to sweep at `before-quit`. The reboot/crash case — the one durability actually cares about — never fires `before-quit`/`beforeunload` anyway, so a quit-time flush wouldn't help it. (Main's `before-quit` still stops the watcher and `closeDb()`s, which checkpoints the SQLite WAL.)
- **Ring buffer size.** Default 64 KiB per session (env override: `CLAUDE_FLEET_BROKER_RING`). Big enough to repaint a screenful of context on reconnect; small enough that 6 workspaces × 6 sessions = 2.3 MiB of buffer is negligible. Revisit if users frequently want more replayed history on reconnect.
- **Interaction with the durable transcript mirror.** Re-attach via broker keeps the same broker session id across long quiet periods (pause + relaunch). The mirror watcher (when it lands) must not treat a long quiet period as a session end.
- **Resource accounting.** `docker pause` freezes processes but doesn't release RAM; an expert workspace pins memory until the container is removed. Several heavy sessions × several expert workspaces can be substantial on a developer machine. Decide whether to surface per-workspace RAM usage in the chip / past-list and whether to warn when the total exceeds a threshold.
- **Pause depth.** "Deep sleep" (allow swap-out, slower wake) vs. "light sleep" (kept resident, instant wake). Out of scope until a user actually needs the differentiation.
- **Broker crash blast radius.** Single multiplexer means a broker bug kills every session in that workspace at once. We accept this tradeoff for the cleaner architecture; mitigate by writing the broker defensively (each session's error handling is local) and by relying on the manager's per-session goroutine isolation. If real crashes appear, revisit the per-session-process model.

### Drag-and-drop file ingestion
Drop OS files, pasted images, web content, or text fragments onto the window; the app saves them into the selected container's workspace so the agent can read them.

**Status: SHIPPED (#87, revived on native Windows).** Originally built then shelved (PR #87, closed unmerged 2026-06-17) because drag-and-drop can't be exercised on the **WSLg dev** setup — OS drag events aren't forwarded Windows→Linux into the Electron window and clipboard image bytes don't reliably cross (a real OS drag event also can't be synthesized in Electron for e2e). It is now revived against a **native Windows build**, where the OS integrations work. Implementation: `src/main/files.ts` + `dropNaming.ts` (all four sources, 100 MB/file + 1 GB/dropbox caps with reject-on-overflow, magic-byte MIME sniffing, collision suffixing, a URL fetch with a 20s abort + streaming size guard, a `*` `.gitignore` in the dropbox); a `useDropIngestion` renderer hook + full-window overlay; results surface through the existing bottom-center toast stack (`pushToast`, with `ok`/`error` variants added alongside the loadout-reload `progress` toast). Unit (`dropNaming.test.ts`) + e2e (`drag-drop.spec.ts`, which drives the `files:*` IPC directly since a real OS drag can't be synthesized). **WSLg caveat retained:** under WSLg dev the overlay still won't receive OS drops; the right-rail Private/Shared folder-reveal links remain the fallback there.

**Decisions made:**
- **Drop target**: anywhere on the window. The file is routed to whichever workspace is currently selected. If none is selected, the drop is rejected with a hint ("Select a workspace first, then drop.").
- **Save location**: `<fleetRoot>/<id>/_dropped/` — the selected workspace's private folder (via `config.ts:fleetPrivateDir`). Filename collisions resolved by suffix (`foo.png`, `foo-2.png`, `foo-3.png`). The host **always** writes here; what differs per backend is the *path string handed back* (next bullet).
- **Returned path is backend-aware (#393)**: the string in the toast/clipboard must be one the agent in that workspace can actually open, which is not the same as the host save path. `localLauncher.ts:agentDropboxPath` maps it by backend: **container** → `/workspace/_dropped/<name>` (the private folder is bind-mounted at `/workspace`); **local, native/custom launcher** → the real host path (claude runs on the host); **local, wsl launcher** → the in-distro view of the host path, via `wslInDistroPath` (a Windows `C:\…\_dropped\<name>` root → its `/mnt/<drive>/…` automount form; a `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\…` root → the plain in-distro path). A hard-coded `/workspace/_dropped/<name>` here was the #393 bug: for a wsl-launcher workspace the dropbox is a Windows path the distro agent only reaches through `/mnt`, so the returned path existed nowhere for it. A wsl mapping that can't produce an absolute-POSIX path **throws** at drop time rather than clipboarding an unopenable string. (The `/mnt/<drive>` translation carries the usual 9P read cost for large drops; moving the dropbox inside the distro is a possible future optimization, not done here.)
- **Post-save behavior**: toast confirmation showing the returned (agent-visible) path, plus the path is copied to the system clipboard (via `clipboard.writeText`). User pastes it into their prompt manually — no auto-typing into the PTY.
- **Sources accepted**:
  - **OS file drag** — drop from Explorer/Finder/Nautilus. Renderer reads the path via `webUtils.getPathForFile(file)`; main copies from source to destination.
  - **Clipboard paste** (Cmd/Ctrl+V) anywhere on the window — image bytes from the clipboard saved as `paste-<ISO-timestamp>.<ext>` (extension derived from clipboard format).
  - **Web drag** — content dragged out of a browser. If a URL, the main process fetches it and writes the body; if inline bytes, written directly. Filename derived from the source URL or Content-Disposition; falls back to `web-<ISO-timestamp>.<ext>`.
  - **Text / HTML drag** — selected text dragged in. Written as `dropped-<ISO-timestamp>.txt` (plain text) or `.html` (when the drag carries HTML).

**IPC surface (as built).** Each takes the selected `workspaceId` and returns the agent-visible saved path(s) (backend-aware, see *Returned path* above):
- `files:dropOsFiles(workspaceId, sourcePaths: string[])` → `string[]` (saved paths) — caps validated across the whole batch before any copy, so a partial over-limit drop writes nothing.
- `files:dropBytes(workspaceId, payload: { suggestedName?, mime?, bytes })` → `string`
- `files:dropUrl(workspaceId, url: string)` → `string`
- `files:dropText(workspaceId, payload: { mime: 'text/plain' | 'text/html', text: string })` → `string`
- `clipboard:readImage()` → `{ bytes, mime } | null` — supports Ctrl+V image ingestion.
